A Reader’s Introduction

Jude is easy to miss. It sits just before Revelation, a single chapter of twenty-five verses, and in the Greek text that underlies this book it runs to about 460 words. Most people could read it aloud in under three minutes. Many Christians know only its closing benediction, “to Him who is able to guard you without stumbling,” and perhaps its call to “fight for the faith once delivered to the holy ones.” The middle of the letter (fallen angels, an archangel quarrelling with the Devil over a corpse, a prophecy from Enoch, reefs, clouds, uprooted trees, and stars that have wandered from their paths) can seem so strange that readers hurry past it.

This book is written for readers who would rather slow down.

Jude rewards slowness because it is so compressed. Almost every phrase calls up a story, a scripture, or a tradition that its first readers knew well and we mostly do not. “The way of Cain” assumes a whole history of how Jewish interpreters understood Cain. “Clouds without water” assumes a farmer’s dread of a rainless sky. “Love feasts” assumes a practice of shared meals in early Christian communities that most churches no longer keep in that form. When Jude names Michael and Moses’ body, or quotes “Enoch, the seventh from Adam,” he speaks the language of a world of Jewish scriptural interpretation that has its own texts, conventions, and arguments. Unfold those references and the letter changes from a cryptic rant into a carefully built piece of pastoral persuasion. It is written with real literary skill, steeped in Israel’s scriptures, and aimed at keeping a community together in the love of God.

What this book is

Jude, Unfolded is an expository commentary: a guided walk through the text, verse by verse and phrase by phrase. It sets out to explain:

  • what the text says, in the words of a very literal English translation and in the Greek behind it;
  • what it likely meant to its first readers, given their scriptures, customs, and situation;
  • why it may have been said that way, which means attending to Jude’s images, his rhetorical patterns, and his choices among traditions;
  • how it may be responsibly applied today, kept clearly separate from the explanation of what the text meant.

Along the way the book stops for deep dives, longer explorations of subjects that modern readers rarely meet elsewhere. A reader who wonders “What exactly was a love feast?” will find a full answer in Chapter 6, drawn from the New Testament and from the earliest Christian and Roman writers who describe such meals. The same care goes to the Book of Enoch, the dispute over Moses’ body, the wandering stars, the “faith once delivered,” and more than a dozen other subjects.

How the chapters work

After the introductory chapters, each of the thirteen main chapters follows the same pattern, so you can always find your way:

  • A. The Text: the passage in the Literal Standard Version.
  • B. Plain-English Orientation: the big picture before the details.
  • C. Phrase-by-Phrase Exploration: the heart of each chapter.
  • D. Original-Language Investigation: the Greek words that matter, explained for readers who know no Greek.
  • E. The Historical and Cultural World: the customs, beliefs, and institutions the text assumes.
  • F. Connections Across Scripture: quotations, allusions, and shared themes, each explained.
  • G. Scholarly and Theological Questions: where interpreters disagree, and why.
  • H. The Passage Within Jude: how this section advances the letter’s argument.
  • I. Contemporary Understanding: possible implications for faith and life today.
  • J. Reflection and Discussion: questions for personal study or groups.
  • K. Sources: the notes behind the chapter’s claims.

You do not need to read every section in order. A small group might read A, B, C, and I together, use J for discussion, and leave D and G to members who want more detail. A preacher or teacher will probably want everything.

Why the Literal Standard Version?

The Literal Standard Version (LSV), published by Covenant Press in 2020, is a very literal modern translation. Its publisher presents it as an improvement on earlier literal versions, including Robert Young’s Literal Translation of the nineteenth century. It keeps closely to the form of the original languages. It preserves verb tenses where it can, marks supplied words in square brackets, renders ἄγγελος consistently as “messenger” and αἰώνιος as “continuous,” and prints “LORD” in capitals in the New Testament where the reference is to the God of Israel. Some of its phrasing is unusual (“those dreaming indeed defile the flesh”; “stars going astray”), and that is part of its value here. A literal translation keeps the seams of the original visible, so we can see what the Greek is doing before we smooth it into natural English. Where the LSV’s literalness hides the sense, this book explains it and compares other translations.

The LSV is also openly licensed (Creative Commons Attribution-ShareAlike), which allows it to be quoted fully and freely. Its publisher asks that its English text not be altered, and this book honors that request: every LSV quotation appears exactly as the LSV has it.

What you will not need

You will not need to know Greek. Every Greek word appears with a transliteration and a plain explanation. You will not need a theological education; technical terms are explained when they first appear and collected in the Glossary. You will not need to agree with every conclusion. Where good interpreters disagree, this book tries to set out the options fairly and to show the evidence on each side, so you can weigh them for yourself.

A word about the tone of Jude

Jude can sound harsh. It speaks of judgment, fire, and darkness, and it describes certain people in vivid and unflattering images. Some readers find this off-putting, and others find it attractive for the wrong reasons. This book takes the severity of Jude seriously without making it the whole story. The letter begins with readers who are “called, … loved, … and … kept,” and it ends with praise to the God who is able to keep them from stumbling. Its last practical words are about mercy, rescue, and compassion for those who are wavering. The darkness of the middle section stands inside a frame of love, and the letter cannot be read rightly without both.

Jude wanted, by his own account, to write about “our common salvation” (v. 3). Circumstances forced him to write a warning instead. Even so, the salvation he wanted to celebrate shines through the warning, and helping you see it is the purpose of this book.

Research Method and Interpretive Principles

A commentary is only as trustworthy as the way it was made. This chapter sets out how this book was researched, what kinds of sources stand behind its claims, and the principles that guided interpretation. Readers who want to get straight to the text can skip ahead and come back later. Readers who want to know how far to trust what follows should read on.

Sources and how they were used

The biblical text. The English text is the Literal Standard Version (LSV), taken from the eBible.org edition and checked word for word against a second rendering of the same edition. The Greek text is the SBL Greek New Testament (SBLGNT), edited by Michael W. Holmes, with its apparatus of variant readings. An updated form of that apparatus records where the Nestle–Aland 28th edition (NA28), the most widely used critical edition today, differs. For comparison the book uses the Robinson–Pierpont edition of the Byzantine text (the form of the Greek found in the majority of later manuscripts), the Textus Receptus (the printed Greek text behind the King James Version), and four public-domain English translations: the WEB, ASV, BSB, and KJV.

Ancient sources. When Jude alludes to a Jewish or early Christian tradition, the book goes back to the ancient text itself wherever an openly available edition exists: 1 Enoch in R. H. Charles’s translation, the Targums in J. W. Etheridge’s translation, Josephus in William Whiston’s, the Septuagint in Brenton’s edition, and the church fathers in the standard nineteenth-century translations. Each passage relied on was read directly. The notes give book, chapter, and section so that readers can check them.

Lexical sources. Greek word meanings are drawn mainly from G. Abbott-Smith’s Manual Greek Lexicon of the New Testament (1922). It is a public-domain lexicon that still repays use, and it summarizes the classical, Septuagint, and papyrus evidence for each New Testament word. Word counts and grammatical data were computed directly from the MorphGNT edition of the SBLGNT, so a statement such as “this word occurs nowhere else in the New Testament” rests on a count of the actual text.

Modern scholarship. The book draws on the main modern commentaries on Jude, above all Richard Bauckham’s, which shaped the modern study of the letter, together with studies of its text, rhetoric, and background. These works could not all be re-consulted page by page during the research for this edition. The book therefore attributes to them only positions they are well known for, cites them by the verse under discussion rather than by page, and identifies them in the Bibliography as works to check before any claim is built on them. This is a deliberate choice: it is better to say less, and say it accurately, than to decorate a sentence with a citation that cannot be traced.

The full source ledger, which records what was verified, how, and what remains uncertain, is part of the project files that accompany the manuscript.

Principles of interpretation

1. The text has the first word. Fidelity to Scripture comes before defending any preferred interpretation. Where a popular reading does not fit Jude’s words, the book says so, even when that reading is cherished.

2. Meaning before application. Each chapter separates what the text meant in its first setting from how it may be applied now. The two are related, but they are not the same thing, and confusing them is the source of much misuse of Jude.

3. Kinds of claims are labeled. The book tries to make clear whether a statement is a textual fact (“the Greek word occurs only here”), historical evidence (“Pliny reports…”), scholarly consensus, a contested interpretation, a theological tradition, a plausible inference, or a suggested application. Historical reconstructions, however likely, are presented as reconstructions.

4. Words mean what they mean in use. A Greek word’s meaning is determined by how it was actually used in its period, not by its etymology or by adding up its parts. The book points out etymologies when they are interesting and warns when they have been overworked. For example, ἐκκλησία does not mean “the called-out ones” just because it contains the parts “out” and “call.” Jude’s word σπιλάδες, discussed in Chapter 6, is a good example of a word whose sense must be settled by usage.

5. Traditions outside the Bible are taken seriously and handled carefully. Jude quotes 1 Enoch and alludes to a tradition about Michael and Moses that appears nowhere in the Old Testament. The book does not explain these away. It shows where they come from, how Jude uses them, and how the church has argued about what that use implies. Quoting a text is not the same as endorsing everything in it, and the book is careful not to claim more than Jude does.

6. Disagreements are presented fairly. When responsible scholars disagree, the book sets out the main options, the evidence for each, and their strengths and weaknesses. It sometimes states a preference and gives reasons, but it tries to give readers what they need to decide differently.

7. No denominational polemics. Christians read Jude within many traditions. Where those traditions differ in ways that affect the meaning, for example on the “brothers” of Jesus or on how believers are “kept,” the book describes the differences without taking sides in old quarrels.

8. The letter stays whole. Word studies serve the reading of the letter and do not replace it. Each chapter ends by placing its passage in the flow of Jude’s argument, and the overview chapter maps the whole.

About the Greek

Greek words appear in Greek script with a transliteration in italics, for example ἀγάπη (agapē). The transliteration follows a simple scholarly system: η = ē, ω = ō, υ = y (u in diphthongs), χ = ch, θ = th, φ = ph, ψ = ps, and rough breathing = h. Readers who do not read Greek can ignore the Greek script and follow the transliteration and explanation. Grammatical terms are explained when they first appear and collected in the Glossary.

A few grammatical ideas recur often enough to introduce here:

  • Tense and aspect. Greek verb forms say less about when an action happens than English tenses do and more about how the action is viewed. The perfect presents a completed action with continuing results (“having been loved,” and still loved). The aorist presents an action as a whole, without comment on its duration. The present presents an action as ongoing or in progress.
  • Participles are verbal adjectives (“having been called,” “praying”). Jude uses them heavily, and much of his meaning rides on them.
  • The optative is a verb form used to express wishes (“may mercy … be multiplied”). It is rare in the New Testament, and Jude uses it twice.
  • Cases (nominative, genitive, dative, accusative) mark a noun’s role in the sentence. The dative in particular can mean “to,” “for,” “by,” or “in,” and Jude’s first verse depends on which is chosen.

About the variant readings

The New Testament survives in thousands of handwritten manuscripts, and they differ in small and sometimes significant ways. Jude has more than its share of interesting variants. One of them changes who is said to have led Israel out of Egypt, and another changes the shape of the letter’s final instructions. The book explains the significant variants where they arise and gathers them in Appendix A. Readers should know that none of these variants touches the substance of the Christian faith. Some do, however, affect how particular verses are read, and they deserve honest explanation.

About application

The “Contemporary Understanding” sections suggest ways Jude’s message may inform belief, worship, fellowship, ethics, and spiritual practice today. They are offered as possibilities to weigh, not as divine instructions. Jude was written against particular people doing particular things, and it is easy to aim its sharpest words at whoever we happen to dislike. The book tries to resist that temptation and to let Jude question its readers, this author included, before it is turned on anyone else.

Historical and Literary Introduction to Jude

Before walking through Jude verse by verse, it helps to ask the questions any careful reader asks of an ancient letter. Who wrote it? When, and to whom? What was happening that made it necessary? What kind of writing is it, and what world of ideas does it assume? How did the early church receive it? For a letter as short as Jude, the answers are often less certain than we would like. The evidence is still worth laying out, both for what it shows and for what it leaves open.

1. “Judas … brother of James”: who wrote the letter?

The letter opens:

Judas, a servant of Jesus Christ, and brother of James, …

— Jude 1, LSV

The Greek name Ἰούδας (Ioudas) is the Greek form of the Hebrew Yehudah, Judah, the name of Jacob’s fourth son and of the tribe and land descended from him. English Bibles usually write “Jude” for the author of this letter. The convention distinguishes him from Judas Iscariot, but the Greek name is the same, and the LSV keeps “Judas.” The name was very common among Jews of the period, so the author’s identity depends on the clue he gives: he is the “brother of James.”

The candidates

Several men named Judas appear in the New Testament:

  1. Judas the brother of Jesus. Mark lists him among Jesus’ brothers: “Is this not the carpenter, the Son of Mary, and brother of James, and Joses, and Judas, and Simon?” (Mark 6:3, LSV; cf. Matt 13:55).
  2. Judas “of James,” one of the Twelve, listed by Luke: “Judas of James, and Judas Iscariot, who also became betrayer” (Luke 6:16, LSV; cf. Acts 1:13). John calls him “Judas … (not the Iscariot)” (John 14:22, LSV). The Greek Ἰούδας Ἰακώβου most naturally means “Judas son of James,” which is the usual way of using a genitive with a personal name. It does not mean “brother of James.”
  3. Judas called Barsabbas, a prophet and leader in the Jerusalem church who carried the apostolic letter to Antioch (Acts 15:22, 32).
  4. Judas Thomas, a figure of later Syriac Christian tradition in which the apostle Thomas (“twin”) is named Judas.

Among these, the first is the only one for whom “brother of James” is a natural self-description. Only one James in early Christianity was famous enough to identify someone simply by naming him: James of Jerusalem, “the brother of the LORD” (Gal 1:19, LSV). Paul names him as one of the “pillars” of the Jerusalem church (Gal 2:9). He leads the council in Acts 15 and receives Paul in Acts 21:18, and tradition takes him to be the author of the Letter of James, who also calls himself simply “a servant of God and of the Lord Jesus Christ” (Jas 1:1, LSV). A Judas who introduces himself as “brother of James” is almost certainly claiming to be the brother of that James, and so a brother of Jesus.

Why “brother of James” and not “brother of the Lord”?

If Jude was the brother of Jesus, why did he not say so? Ancient readers asked the same question. Clement of Alexandria, writing around AD 200, answered with a comment that later readers have found persuasive:

Jude, who wrote the Catholic Epistle, the brother of the sons of Joseph, and very religious, while knowing the near relationship of the Lord, yet did not say that he himself was His brother. But what said he? Jude, a servant of Jesus Christ,—of Him as Lord; but the brother of James.1

Several factors probably worked together. The family relationship was no longer the defining fact; after the resurrection, Jesus’ brothers came to know him as Lord. The Gospels remember that during Jesus’ ministry “not even His brothers were believing in Him” (John 7:5, LSV). Yet after Easter they appear among the praying disciples (Acts 1:14), and Paul knows that the risen Christ “appeared to James” (1 Cor 15:7, LSV). A brother by birth who had become a servant by faith might well prefer to call himself the second. Naming James also placed Jude within a recognized line of authority without claiming the title of apostle, which he appears to reserve for others (v. 17).

  1. Clement of Alexandria, Adumbrationes (Comments) on the Epistle of Jude, in Ante-Nicene Fathers 2, “Fragments of Clemens Alexandrinus,” II; online at https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0211.htm. The comment also identifies Jude as “His brother, (the son) of Joseph.”↩︎

The family of Jesus in early tradition

Two pieces of evidence outside the Gospels fill out the picture.

First, Paul mentions in passing that “the brothers of the LORD” traveled on missionary work with their wives (1 Cor 9:5, LSV). That remark, written in the mid-50s, shows that at least some of Jesus’ brothers besides James were known as traveling preachers among the churches. Jude, who writes to readers he knows and who are at some distance from him, fits that profile.

Second, the second-century Jewish-Christian writer Hegesippus, quoted by Eusebius, tells a story about Jude’s grandsons. When the emperor Domitian (reigned AD 81–96) feared the descendants of David, “the grandchildren of Jude, who is said to have been the Lord’s brother according to the flesh,” were brought before him. They owned a small farm worth nine thousand denarii, showed him the calluses on their hands from working it, and explained that Christ’s kingdom was “not a temporal nor an earthly kingdom, but a heavenly and angelic one.” Domitian let them go as harmless. Afterward, Hegesippus says, they “ruled the churches because they were witnesses and were also relatives of the Lord,” and they lived until the time of Trajan.2 The story probably includes some legendary color, but it preserves a memory of Jude’s family as Galilean farmers, rooted in Jewish Christianity and respected in the churches.

  1. Eusebius, Ecclesiastical History 3.19–20, quoting Hegesippus (NPNF² 1; https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/250103.htm).↩︎

Deep dive: who were Jesus’ “brothers”?

The New Testament names four brothers of Jesus (James, Joses or Joseph, Judas, and Simon) and mentions sisters (Mark 6:3). Christians have understood these relationships in three main ways, and the differences matter for how readers picture Jude.

  • Full brothers (the “Helvidian” view, after Helvidius, a fourth-century writer). On this view they were children of Mary and Joseph born after Jesus. Most Protestant interpreters hold it, pointing to the plain sense of the word ἀδελφός (adelphos) and to texts such as Matt 1:25.
  • Older half-brothers, sons of Joseph by an earlier marriage (the “Epiphanian” view, after Epiphanius of Salamis). This view is attested in second-century writings. It has been common in Eastern Orthodox tradition and fits Clement’s description of Jude as “the brother of the sons of Joseph.”
  • Cousins (the “Hieronymian” view, after Jerome). This view takes adelphos in the broader sense of “kinsman,” a sense it can carry in Jewish usage. It became standard in the Latin West and is common in Roman Catholic interpretation.

All three views agree on what matters most for this book: Jude belonged to Jesus’ own family circle, grew up near him, and came to call him “Master and Lord” (v. 4). Readers who hold different views of Mary can read Jude in the same way.

Did Jude himself write it?

Many scholars accept that the letter was written by Jude, the brother of Jesus. Many others think it was written later by someone using his name, a practice called pseudonymity that was known in the ancient world. The main arguments for a later writer are these:

  • The Greek is good. Jude’s vocabulary is rich, with sixteen words found nowhere else in the New Testament, and his style is vivid and carefully patterned. Could a Galilean villager write like this?
  • “The faith once delivered” (v. 3) is said to treat Christianity as a fixed body of doctrine, which some regard as typical of a later, more institutional stage.
  • The apostles seem to belong to the past (v. 17).

Defenders of authenticity reply:

  • Greek was widely used in Galilee, and a family whose members traveled as missionaries in the Greek-speaking world (1 Cor 9:5) had every opportunity to learn it well. Ancient authors also commonly used secretaries, who could polish style. The quality of the Greek is a real question, but it does not settle authorship.
  • “The faith” as the content of the Christian message already appears in Paul’s earliest letters: “he who is persecuting us then, now proclaims good news—the faith that he was then destroying” (Gal 1:23, LSV). Language about “delivering” teaching is also early (1 Cor 11:2; 15:3).
  • Verse 17 asks readers to remember what the apostles told them. That requires only that the apostles had preached to these readers earlier, not that they were all long dead.
  • The choice of name is the strongest argument. A later writer who wanted authority would hardly choose an obscure figure like Jude, and having chosen him, would likely have added the impressive title “brother of the Lord.” A modest self-description fits the real Jude better than an imitator.

This book treats authorship by Jude the brother of Jesus as a responsible conclusion that fits the evidence well. It does not regard the question as closed, and very little of the letter’s interpretation depends on the answer.3

  1. For a sustained case for authenticity, see Richard Bauckham, Jude, 2 Peter (WBC 50; Waco: Word, 1983), introduction, and his Jude and the Relatives of Jesus in the Early Church (Edinburgh: T&T Clark, 1990). For views favoring a later date and pseudonymous authorship, see the introductions in J. N. D. Kelly, A Commentary on the Epistles of Peter and of Jude (London: A&C Black, 1969), and Jerome H. Neyrey, 2 Peter, Jude (AB 37C; New York: Doubleday, 1993). (These works are cited from prior knowledge; see the Bibliography.)↩︎

2. Language and style

Jude writes a compact, energetic, and artful Greek. Several features stand out, and they will recur throughout this book:

  • Rare words. Of about 226 distinct words in Jude, sixteen occur nowhere else in the New Testament, among them the words meaning ‘contend for’ (v. 3), ‘slip in’ (v. 4), ‘reefs’ (v. 12), ‘late-autumn’ (v. 12), ‘foam up’ (v. 13), ‘wanderers’ (v. 13), ‘grumblers’ and ‘fault-finders’ (v. 16), ‘make divisions’ (v. 19), and ‘without stumbling’ (v. 24).4
  • Triads. Jude thinks in threes: called, loved, kept (v. 1); mercy, peace, love (v. 2); three examples of judgment (vv. 5–7); three sins of the ‘dreamers’ (v. 8); Cain, Balaam, Korah (v. 11); three groups to whom mercy is shown (vv. 22–23).
  • Catchwords. Key words return in new settings and bind the letter together. The clearest is “keep” (τηρέω, tēreō): the readers are “kept” (v. 1), the angels did not “keep” their position and are “kept” for judgment (v. 6), darkness is “kept” for the wandering stars (v. 13), and the readers are to “keep” themselves in God’s love (v. 21) as they trust the God who is able to “guard” them (v. 24).
  • The “these” pattern. After each example or prophecy, Jude turns to the troublemakers with “these” (οὗτοι, houtoi): “those dreaming” (v. 8, where the LSV renders the word “those”), “these, indeed, whatever [things] they have not known” (v. 10), “These are stains” (v. 12), “These are … grumblers” (v. 16), “these are those causing divisions” (v. 19).
  • Vivid images drawn from nature and from Israel’s story.

  1. Counts computed from the SBLGNT with MorphGNT lemmatization: 459 words, 226 distinct lemmas, 16 lemmas unique to Jude within the New Testament. See the project file research/data-notes.md. A different edition or lemmatization would change the figures slightly.↩︎

3. Date and setting

The letter gives no explicit date. If Jude the brother of Jesus wrote it, it was written within his lifetime, probably before the 90s and possibly much earlier, since Hegesippus’s story about Domitian concerns his grandsons. Some scholars date it to the 50s or early 60s, which would make it one of the earliest New Testament writings. Others place it between 70 and 100. The letter’s horizon is strikingly Jewish and apocalyptic. It never mentions the destruction of Jerusalem’s temple (AD 70), but arguments from silence are weak in so short a letter.

This uncertainty affects the interpretation less than one might expect. Jude’s concerns are about holding to the received faith, moral integrity, authority, divisive teachers, and mercy toward the wavering. Those concerns fit the first Christian century at almost any point.

4. The recipients

Jude addresses “those called, having been loved … and having been kept” (v. 1), a description of all believers rather than of a particular place. Yet the letter is clearly written to particular people: he calls them “beloved” three times (vv. 3, 17, 20). He knows that intruders have slipped into their community (v. 4) and sit at their love feasts (v. 12), and he can remind them of what the apostles had told them (vv. 17–18).

He also assumes that they will understand compressed references to the exodus, to fallen angels, to Michael and Moses, to Cain, Balaam, and Korah, and to the prophecy of Enoch. Such readers were most likely Jewish Christians, or a mixed community deeply formed by Jewish scriptural interpretation. Suggested locations include Palestine, Syria, Egypt, and Asia Minor. None can be proven, and this book does not build on any of them.

5. The troublemakers

Jude does not name the people he opposes. He calls them “certain men” (v. 4), and everything we know about them comes from his description. Gathering his charges gives this profile:

  • They are inside the community. They “came in … stealthily” (v. 4) and share its meals (v. 12).
  • They turn grace into license, especially sexual license (vv. 4, 7–8, 16, 18).
  • They deny Jesus Christ as Master and Lord, probably by the way they live rather than by explicit doctrinal denial (v. 4).
  • They appeal to dreams, apparently claimed revelations (v. 8).
  • They reject authority and slander glorious beings (vv. 8–10).
  • They are self-serving, “shepherding themselves” (v. 12), “for the sake of profit” (v. 16), like Balaam “for reward” (v. 11).
  • They are divisive and, in Jude’s judgment, “not having the Spirit” (v. 19).

Older commentators often identified them as Gnostics, members of the second-century movements that taught salvation through secret knowledge and despised the material world. The resemblance is partial at best. Jude says nothing about a lower creator-god, a cosmic myth, or a doctrine of secret knowledge. The developed Gnostic systems also belong to a later period than most scholars assign to Jude. A more cautious description, widely held since Richard Bauckham’s commentary, sees them as traveling teachers who claimed prophetic or charismatic authority and taught that grace freed believers from moral law.5 That is itself a reconstruction, and the book treats it as one.

  1. Bauckham, Jude, 2 Peter, introduction (“The Opponents”). The “Gnostic” identification was common in nineteenth- and early twentieth-century scholarship.↩︎

6. What kind of writing is Jude?

Jude is a letter. It has the standard opening (sender, recipients, greeting) and an elaborate closing doxology in place of the usual farewell. In substance it is a sermon or word of exhortation in letter form, with a clear rhetorical aim: to move its hearers to “fight for the faith” (v. 3).

Two analyses of its structure have been especially influential:

  • A Jewish interpretive pattern. Scholars such as E. Earle Ellis and Richard Bauckham observed that the central section (vv. 5–19) alternates between texts, meaning scriptural examples, a tradition, a prophecy, and an apostolic prediction, and interpretations introduced by “these.” The pattern resembles the commentary method known from the Dead Sea Scrolls, in which a biblical text is quoted and then applied to the present (“its interpretation concerns…”). It suggests a writer trained in Jewish ways of reading Scripture as prophecy fulfilled in his own time.6
  • Greco-Roman rhetoric. Duane F. Watson read Jude according to the conventions of ancient persuasive speech: an introduction stating the purpose (v. 3), a statement of the situation (v. 4), the main proofs (vv. 5–16), and a concluding appeal (vv. 17–23), framed by the letter opening and doxology.7

The two analyses do not compete. A Jewish author writing in Greek could use both inherited Jewish methods and the persuasive patterns any educated speaker of Greek would know.

  1. E. Earle Ellis, “Prophecy and Hermeneutic in Jude,” in Prophecy and Hermeneutic in Early Christianity (Tübingen: Mohr Siebeck, 1978); Bauckham, Jude, 2 Peter, on vv. 5–19.↩︎

  2. Duane F. Watson, Invention, Arrangement, and Style: Rhetorical Criticism of Jude and 2 Peter (SBLDS 104; Atlanta: Scholars Press, 1988).↩︎

7. Jude’s scriptural world

Jude is saturated with Israel’s scriptures, yet he never introduces an Old Testament quotation with “it is written.” His one formal quotation comes from 1 Enoch (vv. 14–15). His references to the Hebrew Bible are compressed allusions that assume the reader knows the story:

Jude Allusion
5 The exodus and the wilderness unbelief (Exod 12–14; Num 14)
6 The “sons of God” (Gen 6:1–4) as read in 1 Enoch 6–16
7 Sodom and Gomorrah (Gen 19)
9 Michael and Moses’ burial (Deut 34:5–6) via later tradition; Michael’s words echo Zech 3:2 (“YHWH pushes against you, O Satan”)
11 Cain (Gen 4), Balaam (Num 22–24; 31:16), Korah (Num 16)
12 Shepherds feeding themselves (Ezek 34:2); clouds and wind without rain (Prov 25:14)
13 The restless sea casting up mire (Isa 57:20)
14–15 Enoch (Gen 5:21–24); 1 Enoch 1:9
16 The wilderness grumbling (Exod 16; Num 14)
23 A brand snatched from the fire; filthy garments (Zech 3:2–4; Amos 4:11)

One detail suggests that Jude knew his scriptures in Hebrew or Aramaic rather than only in the Greek Septuagint. In Isa 57:20 and Prov 25:14, the passages behind his images of the foaming sea and the rainless clouds, the Hebrew text contains the very details Jude uses: the sea “casts out filth and mire,” and there are “clouds and wind without rain” (LSV, translating the Hebrew). The Septuagint versions of both verses lack those details.8 This is not proof, but it fits a Palestinian Jewish author.

Jude also draws on Jewish traditions outside the Hebrew Bible: 1 Enoch, the fullest surviving source for the Watcher story and for the prophecy he quotes, and a tradition about Michael’s dispute with the Devil over Moses’ body, which early Christian writers associated with a work called the Assumption (or Ascension) of Moses. These references were the main reason some early Christians hesitated over Jude. They are discussed fully in Chapters 3, 4, and 8.

  1. Compare the Septuagint of Isa 57:20 (Οἱ δὲ ἄδικοι κλυδωνισθήσονται, καὶ ἀναπαύσασθαι οὐ δυνήσονται, “the unrighteous will be tossed like waves and will not be able to rest”), which lacks the mire, and LXX Prov 25:14, which differs substantially from the Hebrew. Greek text from Brenton’s Septuagint, eBible.org edition. Bauckham makes the broader argument that Jude’s allusions reflect the Hebrew text (Jude, 2 Peter, introduction and on vv. 12–13).↩︎

8. Jude and 2 Peter

Anyone who reads Jude and then 2 Peter 2 notices the resemblance. The same sequence of themes and images appears in both: intruders who deny the Master (Jude 4; 2 Pet 2:1), sinning angels kept in gloom for judgment (Jude 6; 2 Pet 2:4), Sodom and Gomorrah (Jude 7; 2 Pet 2:6), defiling the flesh and despising authority (Jude 8; 2 Pet 2:10), angels who do not bring a slanderous judgment (Jude 9; 2 Pet 2:11), creatures without reason (Jude 10; 2 Pet 2:12), Balaam’s love of reward (Jude 11; 2 Pet 2:15), feasting together (Jude 12; 2 Pet 2:13), waterless clouds and gloom of darkness (Jude 12–13; 2 Pet 2:17), “swollen” words (Jude 16; 2 Pet 2:18), and scoffers foretold by the apostles (Jude 17–18; 2 Pet 3:2–3). Three Greek words occur in the New Testament only in these two letters.

There are three main explanations:

  1. 2 Peter used Jude. This is the majority view. Jude’s version is usually the more compact and more Jewish, and it is easier to explain why 2 Peter would omit Jude’s quotation from Enoch and his named Michael story than why Jude would add them. 2 Peter also arranges the examples in biblical order (angels, flood, Sodom), while Jude’s order follows his own thematic logic.
  2. Jude used 2 Peter. A minority view, defended by older scholars such as Charles Bigg. It points to 2 Peter’s future tense (“there will be false teachers,” 2 Pet 2:1) and Jude’s past tense (“there came in,” Jude 4), as if Jude were reporting the fulfillment of 2 Peter’s prediction.
  3. Both used a common source, perhaps a body of early Christian teaching about false teachers.

The question matters for the study of 2 Peter more than for Jude. This book reads Jude on its own terms and uses 2 Peter as an early interpreter of Jude’s language. Appendix C gives the parallel texts side by side.

9. How the church received Jude

Jude’s place in the New Testament was secure early in some regions and debated in others, and the debate centered on his use of Enoch.

  • Late second century: the Muratorian Fragment, a Latin list of accepted books whose date is debated, includes “the Epistle of Jude.”9 Clement of Alexandria commented on Jude in his Hypotyposes, and part of that commentary survives in Latin.10 Tertullian, around AD 200, used Jude to defend Enoch’s authority: “Enoch possesses a testimony in the Apostle Jude.”11
  • Third century: Origen praised Jude as “a letter of few lines, it is true, but filled with the healthful words of heavenly grace.”12
  • Early fourth century: Eusebius placed Jude among the “disputed writings, which are nevertheless recognized by many,” noting that “not many of the ancients have mentioned it” but that it had been “read publicly in very many churches.”13
  • AD 367: Athanasius’s Easter letter listing the books of the New Testament includes “one of Jude” among the seven catholic epistles.14
  • Late fourth century: Jerome explained the hesitation directly: “because in it he quotes from the apocryphal Book of Enoch it is rejected by many. Nevertheless by age and use it has gained authority and is reckoned among the Holy Scriptures.”15

At the Reformation, Martin Luther valued the letter but placed it among the books “that need not be counted among the chief books,” calling it “an extract or copy of St. Peter’s second epistle.”16 John Calvin defended its authority and suggested that Jude drew on ancient tradition, not necessarily on an apocryphal book.17

One further fact helps modern readers understand the issue. The Book of Enoch, which Jude quotes, is itself part of the Old Testament canon of the Ethiopian Orthodox Tewahedo Church and the Eritrean Orthodox Tewahedo Church, though not of other churches or of Judaism.18 The question “What does it mean that Jude quotes Enoch?” therefore receives different answers in different Christian traditions. Chapter 8 explores it.

  1. Muratorian Fragment, in Ante-Nicene Fathers 5, “Fragments of Caius,” §4 (https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0510.htm).↩︎

  2. Eusebius, Ecclesiastical History 6.14.1 (https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/250106.htm); Clement, Adumbrationes (see n. 1).↩︎

  3. Tertullian, On the Apparel of Women (De cultu feminarum) 1.3 (ANF 4; https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0402.htm).↩︎

  4. Origen, Commentary on Matthew 10.17 (ANF 9; https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/101610.htm).↩︎

  5. Eusebius, Ecclesiastical History 3.25.3 and 2.23.25 (https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/250103.htm, https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/250102.htm).↩︎

  6. Athanasius, Festal Letter 39.5 (NPNF² 4; https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/2806039.htm).↩︎

  7. Jerome, On Illustrious Men (De viris illustribus) 4 (NPNF² 3; https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/2708.htm).↩︎

  8. Martin Luther, “Preface to the Epistles of St. James and St. Jude” (1522), English translation as reproduced at https://www.bible-researcher.com/antilegomena.html (from Luther’s Works, vol. 35).↩︎

  9. John Calvin, Commentaries on the Catholic Epistles, trans. John Owen (Edinburgh: Calvin Translation Society, 1855), on Jude 9 and 14 (CCEL edition).↩︎

  10. On the canonical status and textual history of 1 Enoch, see the summary in “Book of Enoch,” Wikipedia (accessed 7 November 2026), and George W. E. Nickelsburg, 1 Enoch 1 (Hermeneia; Minneapolis: Fortress, 2001), introduction (cited from prior knowledge).↩︎

10. Why Jude matters

Jude has been called the most neglected book of the New Testament. Its neglect is understandable but costly. In twenty-five verses it offers:

  • a picture of Christian identity as gift before task: called, loved, kept;
  • a clear statement that the gospel has content, entrusted once for all and worth contending for;
  • an unflinching moral seriousness about grace twisted into license;
  • a demonstration of how the first Christians read Israel’s scriptures and traditions as speaking to their present;
  • a remarkably early and high Christology: Jesus is “our only Master and Lord,” possibly the one who saved Israel from Egypt, and the coming Lord of Enoch’s prophecy;
  • a model of contending without contempt, defending the faith while showing mercy to the wavering and rescuing those in danger;
  • one of the most beautiful doxologies in Scripture, which turns the whole letter toward the God who keeps his people.

The chapters that follow try to show how these pieces fit together.

The Whole Letter at a Glance

Jude is short enough to read in one sitting, and it should be read that way before it is taken apart. Its first hearers would have heard it read aloud as a whole, probably at a gathering of the church. Read it now, slowly, ideally aloud.

The Epistle of Jude in the Literal Standard Version

1 Judas, a servant of Jesus Christ, and brother of James, to those called, having been loved in God [the] Father, and having been kept in Jesus Christ, 2 mercy and peace and love be multiplied to you!

3 Beloved, using all diligence to write to you concerning our common salvation, I had necessity to write to you, exhorting [you] to fight for the faith once delivered to the holy ones, 4 for there came in certain men stealthily, having been written beforehand to this judgment long ago, impious, perverting the grace of our God to licentiousness, and denying our only Master and Lord—Jesus Christ.

5 Now I intend to remind you, you once having known all this, that Jesus, having saved a people out of the land of Egypt, He secondly destroyed those not having believed; 6 messengers also, those who did not keep their own principality, but left their proper dwelling, He has kept in eternal bonds under darkness until [the] judgment of [the] great day, 7 as Sodom and Gomorrah, and the cities around them, in like manner to these [messengers], having given themselves to whoredom, and having gone after other flesh, have been set before [as] an example, undergoing the justice of continuous fire.

8 In like manner, nevertheless, those dreaming indeed defile the flesh, and they disregard lordship, and they speak evil of glorious [ones], 9 yet Michael, the chief messenger, when disputing with the Devil, reasoning about the body of Moses, did not dare to bring against [him] a judgment of slander, but said, “The LORD rebuke you!” 10 But these, indeed, whatever [things] they have not known, they speak evil of; and whatever [they know], like the irrational beasts, they understand naturally—in these they are corrupted; 11 woe to them! Because they went on in the way of Cain, and they rushed to the deceit of Balaam for reward, and in the controversy of Korah they perished.

12 These are stains at your love-feasts, feasting together with you without fear, shepherding themselves; waterless clouds, being carried away by winds; autumnal trees without fruit, having died twice, having been uprooted; 13 wild waves of a sea, foaming up their own shameful [deeds]; stars going astray, for whom the gloom of the darkness throughout the age has been kept.

14 And Enoch, the seventh from Adam, also prophesied to these, saying, “Behold, the LORD has come with myriads of His holy ones, 15 to do judgment against all, and to convict all their impious ones, concerning all their works of impiety that they did impiously, and concerning all the harsh [words] that impious sinners spoke against Him.” 16 These are discontented grumblers, walking according to their own lusts; and their mouth speaks swollen words, giving admiration to persons for the sake of profit;

17 but you, beloved, remember the sayings spoken before by the apostles of our Lord Jesus Christ, 18 that they said to you, that in the last time there will be scoffers, following after their own lusts of impieties, 19 these are those causing divisions, natural men, not having the Spirit.

20 But you, beloved, building yourselves up on your most holy faith, praying in the Holy Spirit, 21 keep yourselves in the love of God, waiting for the mercy of our Lord Jesus Christ [resulting] in continuous life; 22 and, indeed, show mercy to those who are doubting, 23 and rescue others by snatching [them] out of fire; but show mercy to others in fear, hating even the coat having been stained from the flesh.

24 And to Him who is able to guard you without stumbling, and to set [you] in the presence of His glory unblemished, in gladness, 25 to the only wise God our Savior, [is] glory and greatness, power and authority, both now and forever! Amen.

— Jude 1–25, LSV

The shape of the letter

Read as a whole, Jude has a clear architecture: a bright frame around a dark center.

Section Verses Content Key words
Greeting 1–2 Sender; recipients called, loved, kept; wish for mercy, peace, love kept, love, mercy
Occasion and appeal 3–4 Jude meant to write about salvation, but must urge readers to contend for the faith, because intruders have slipped in beloved, faith, impious
The case against the intruders 5–19 Five “texts,” each followed by “these” applying it to the intruders these, impious, judgment, kept
— first text 5–7 Three examples of judgment: wilderness generation, angels, Sodom
— application 8 “those dreaming…”
— second text 9 Michael’s restraint in the dispute over Moses’ body
— application 10–13 “these … speak evil”; woe (Cain, Balaam, Korah); images from nature
— third text 14–15 Enoch’s prophecy of the Lord’s coming with holy myriads
— application 16 “These are … grumblers”
— fourth text 17–18 The apostles’ prediction of scoffers
— application 19 “these are those causing divisions”
Exhortation 20–23 Build, pray, keep yourselves, wait; show mercy, rescue, show mercy with fear beloved, keep, love, mercy
Doxology 24–25 Praise to the God who is able to guard and present his people guard, glory

(Some outlines count the woe of v. 11 as a separate “text” and vv. 12–13 as its application, giving five texts in all. The structure is the same either way.)

Three features of this design are worth carrying into every chapter.

1. The frame interprets the center

The darkest material in the letter, judgment, darkness, and fire, sits between a greeting that calls the readers loved and kept and a doxology that praises God for keeping them from stumbling. The structure itself makes a theological claim: the readers’ security does not depend on the absence of danger. The danger is real, but the one who keeps them is greater. A reading of Jude that remembers only the middle has misread the letter.

2. “Keep” runs through the whole

The Greek verb τηρέω (tēreō, “keep, guard, preserve”) appears five times, and its near relative φυλάσσω (phylassō, “guard”) once more at the climax:

Verse LSV Who keeps what
1 “having been kept in Jesus Christ” God keeps the believers
6 “those who did not keep their own principality” Angels failed to keep their position
6 “He has kept in eternal bonds” God keeps the rebel angels for judgment
13 “the gloom of the darkness … has been kept” Darkness is kept for the wanderers
21 “keep yourselves in the love of God” Believers are to keep themselves
24 “Him who is able to guard you” God guards the believers

Two kinds of keeping run through the letter: protective keeping for those who belong to God, and custodial keeping for those who rebel. The angels “did not keep” what was theirs, and so they are “kept” for judgment. The readers are “kept” by God, and so they are to “keep” themselves in his love. The whole letter turns on this wordplay.

3. “Beloved” vs. “these”

Jude addresses his readers as “beloved” (ἀγαπητοί, agapētoi) three times (vv. 3, 17, 20). Each time the address marks a turning point toward them. He refers to the intruders as “these” (οὗτοι, houtoi) five times (vv. 8, 10, 12, 16, 19), each time applying an ancient example to them. The letter moves between the two groups, and its rhetoric depends on the contrast. In vv. 22–23, however, the line between them becomes porous. Some who have been influenced by the intruders can still be shown mercy and snatched from the fire. Jude’s contrast exists to protect people, not to write them off.

Reading strategy for the chapters ahead

As you work through the commentary, it may help to keep three questions in mind for each passage:

  1. What is Jude asking his readers to see? Much of the letter is an argument from examples: “remember,” “behold,” “these are.”
  2. What is Jude asking his readers to do? The commands are few but weighty: contend (v. 3), remember (v. 17), build, pray, keep, wait (vv. 20–21), show mercy, rescue (vv. 22–23).
  3. What is Jude asking his readers to trust? The letter begins and ends with God’s keeping. Everything between is written by someone who believes God is able.

Part One: The Commentary

Chapter 1. Called, Loved, and Kept: Identity and Greeting (Jude 1–2)

A. The Text

1 Judas, a servant of Jesus Christ, and brother of James, to those called, having been loved in God [the] Father, and having been kept in Jesus Christ, 2 mercy and peace and love be multiplied to you!

— Jude 1–2, LSV

B. Plain-English Orientation

Ancient letters opened with a standard formula: From A, to B, greetings. Jude follows the pattern, but he fills each part with meaning. He introduces himself by two relationships, servant of Jesus Christ and brother of James. He describes his readers by three things God has done for them: God has called them, loved them, and kept them. Then he wishes them three gifts in abundance: mercy, peace, and love.

These two verses are easy to skim on the way to the “real” content of the letter. They are, however, the foundation on which everything else stands. Before Jude says a word about danger, he tells his readers who they are and whose they are. The letter will speak of people who “did not keep” their place and are “kept” for judgment, and it will urge the readers to “keep” themselves in the love of God. All of that is anchored here, in a description of believers as people who have already been loved and kept.

C. Phrase-by-Phrase Exploration

“Judas”

The author’s name is the Greek form of Judah, the patriarch whose name became the name of a tribe, a kingdom, and finally the Jewish people as a whole. For a Jewish family in Galilee, naming a son Judah was a patriotic and pious act. The name also recalled Judas Maccabeus, the hero of the revolt against Antiochus IV in the second century BC. Among Jews of the first century it was one of the most common names.

English Bibles traditionally write “Jude” for the author of this letter and “Judas” for others, partly so that readers do not confuse him with Judas Iscariot. The LSV, true to its literal approach, keeps “Judas.” The Greek makes no distinction. As the Historical Introduction explains, the author is almost certainly the Judas named among the brothers of Jesus in Mark 6:3.

“a servant of Jesus Christ”

The Greek word is δοῦλος (doulos), the ordinary word for a slave. The LSV’s “servant” is a long-standing English convention, but it softens the word. In the Roman world a doulos was not an employee who could resign. A doulos was a person owned by another, whose time, labor, and body belonged to a master.

Two backgrounds give the phrase its force.

The first is the Old Testament phrase “servant of the LORD” (in Hebrew ʿebed YHWH). It was a title of honor, given to Moses (“My servant Moses is dead,” Josh 1:2, LSV), to David (“Go, and you have said to My servant, to David,” 2 Sam 7:5, LSV), and to the prophets. To be someone’s servant was to be identified with that person’s authority and to act on his behalf. The Septuagint uses δοῦλος almost always to translate the Hebrew ʿebed.19

The second is Christian usage. Paul begins Romans with “Paul, a servant of Jesus Christ” (Rom 1:1, LSV), and James begins his letter “James, a servant of God and of the Lord Jesus Christ” (Jas 1:1, LSV). Paul could also describe Christ himself as one who “emptied Himself, having taken the form of a servant” (Phil 2:7, LSV), using the same word, doulos.

For Jude the title says something personal. He grew up in the same household as Jesus. He might have presented himself as an equal, or at least as a relative with special standing. Instead he calls himself Jesus’ slave. The family tie remains, but it has been taken up into a new relationship of total belonging, the same belonging that will mark all his readers. A few lines later he will call Jesus “our only Master” (v. 4), and the Greek word there, δεσπότης (despotēs), is the normal word for a slave’s owner. Jude is consistent: if Jesus is the despotēs, Jude is his doulos.

  1. Abbott-Smith, Manual Greek Lexicon, s.v. δοῦλος: “in LXX, ὁ δ. nearly always for עֶבֶד.”↩︎

“and brother of James”

The second relationship identifies Jude for his readers by naming his famous brother, James, the leader of the Jerusalem church (Gal 1:19; 2:9; Acts 15:13). As the Historical Introduction explains, early readers like Clement of Alexandria saw humility in Jude’s choice to name James rather than Jesus.20 The phrase also establishes credentials. Jude speaks as a member of a family whose authority the churches recognized, without claiming to be an apostle.

  1. Clement of Alexandria, Adumbrationes on Jude, ANF 2 (https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0211.htm): Jude, “while knowing the near relationship of the Lord, yet did not say that he himself was His brother.”↩︎

“to those called”

In Greek the description of the readers is built so that the key word comes last: to-the-in-God-the-Father-loved-and-for-Jesus-Christ-kept called-ones. The final word, κλητοῖς (klētois), “called,” is the noun the other phrases describe. It is the readers’ basic identity: they are people God has called.

“Calling” in the Bible is not a general invitation that people may take or leave. It is God’s effective summons that brings a people into being. God called Abraham out of Ur, and he called Israel out of Egypt. In Isaiah, God says to his servant, “I, YHWH, called you in righteousness, / And I lay hold on your hand, and keep you” (Isa 42:6, LSV). There calling and keeping stand side by side, as they do in Jude.

The New Testament applies this language to the church. Paul writes to “the called of Jesus Christ … called holy ones” (Rom 1:6–7, LSV), and to “those called—both Jews and Greeks” (1 Cor 1:24, LSV). Revelation pictures the Lamb’s companions as those who “are called, and chosen, and steadfast” (Rev 17:14, LSV). By placing “called” in the climactic position, Jude says that his readers’ existence as a community begins with God’s initiative, not their own.

“having been loved in God [the] Father”

The Greek participle ἠγαπημένοις (ēgapēmenois) is in the perfect tense, which presents a past action with continuing results. Its sense is “having been loved and remaining loved.” The love Jude has in mind is not a passing feeling but a settled state.

The phrase “in God [the] Father” is unusual. Most often the New Testament speaks of being loved by God. Some interpreters take the Greek preposition ἐν (en) here in an instrumental sense, “loved by God the Father.” Others keep the local sense, “loved in God,” meaning that believers are held within the sphere of God’s own love. The LSV keeps the literal “in” and lets the reader feel its strangeness. Either way, the source of the love is God the Father, and it is the same love in which the readers are later told to “keep” themselves (v. 21).

The Old Testament roots of this idea are deep. Moses told Israel that God did not choose them for their size or merit, “but because of YHWH’s loving you, and because of His keeping the oath” (Deut 7:8, LSV). Love and keeping stand together there too.

A variant reading. Many later Greek manuscripts read ἡγιασμένοις (hēgiasmenois), “sanctified,” in place of ἠγαπημένοις, “loved.” The King James Version follows this reading (“to them that are sanctified by God the Father”), as does the WEB, which translates the Majority Text. The critical editions (Westcott–Hort, Tregelles, NA28, and SBLGNT) all read “loved,” and the LSV, though it generally follows the Majority Text, does so here too. The likely explanation is that a scribe found “loved in God” awkward and replaced it with the more familiar “sanctified in,” which echoes 1 Cor 1:2 (“sanctified in Christ Jesus”). This variant also illustrates a basic principle of textual criticism. According to the manuscript tallies in one modern edition, “sanctified” appears in about 90 percent of surviving manuscripts and “loved” in about 9 percent.21 Most of those manuscripts, however, are late copies of a single textual tradition. The editors of the critical texts judged that the reading of the earliest witnesses, and the harder reading that best explains its rival, is more likely original. Counting manuscripts is not the same as weighing them.

  1. The Text-Critical Greek New Testament notes (Robert Adam Boyd; eBible.org grctcgnt) give ἡγιασμένοις 90.2% and ἠγαπημένοις 8.9% of manuscripts. These figures count manuscripts and do not weigh them; they are cited only to illustrate the distribution. The critical editions’ choice of ἠγαπημένοις is recorded in the SBLGNT apparatus (WH, Treg, NIV, NA28 against RP).↩︎

“and having been kept in Jesus Christ”

Again the participle is perfect: τετηρημένοις (tetērēmenois), “having been kept and remaining kept.” The verb τηρέω (tēreō) means “to watch over, guard, keep, preserve.”22 It will become the central word of the letter.

The Greek has no preposition here. It reads simply “to-Jesus-Christ kept,” with “Jesus Christ” in the dative case. Because the Greek dative can express several relationships, three readings are possible:

  1. “kept for Jesus Christ” (WEB, ASV, and most modern translations). Believers are kept safe for Christ, held in trust until his return. This fits Paul’s prayer that believers’ “whole spirit and soul and body be preserved, unblameably at the coming of our Lord Jesus Christ” (1 Thess 5:23, LSV), and the letter’s own climax, where God sets believers before his glory (v. 24).
  2. “kept by Jesus Christ.” Christ himself is the keeper. Compare Jesus’ prayer, “when I was with them in the world, I was keeping them in Your Name” (John 17:12, LSV).
  3. “kept in Jesus Christ” (LSV). The preposition “in” from the previous phrase is understood again, so that believers are kept within their union with Christ.

The first reading is the most natural sense of a dative of this kind, and it fits the forward-looking shape of the letter, which ends with believers presented before God’s glory. The other readings are possible, and all three are true to the New Testament’s wider teaching. Jude’s emphasis is clear in any case: the readers’ safety is something God has done and is doing.

The word will return with sharp irony. In v. 6, angels who “did not keep” (μὴ τηρήσαντας, mē tērēsantas) their position are “kept” (τετήρηκεν, tetērēken) in bonds for judgment. In v. 13, the gloom of darkness “has been kept” (τετήρηται, tetērētai) for the wandering stars. In v. 21, the readers are told, “keep yourselves” (τηρήσατε, tērēsate). From the opening line, Jude is preparing a contrast between two kinds of keeping: keeping for glory and keeping for judgment.

  1. Abbott-Smith, s.v. τηρέω: “to watch over, guard, keep, preserve.”↩︎

“mercy and peace and love”

Most New Testament letters greet their readers with “grace and peace.” The pastoral letters to Timothy and 2 John have “grace, mercy, peace.” Only Jude has mercy, peace, and love.

  • Mercy (ἔλεος, eleos) is God’s compassion toward the needy and undeserving. In the Septuagint it most often translates the Hebrew ḥesed, God’s steadfast covenant love and loyalty.23 Jude will mention mercy three more times: the readers are to wait for “the mercy of our Lord Jesus Christ” (v. 21), and they are to “show mercy” to others (vv. 22–23). The letter begins with mercy given and ends with mercy passed on.
  • Peace (εἰρήνη, eirēnē) carries the full sense of the Hebrew shalom: wholeness, safety, right relationship with God and others. In the Septuagint, eirēnē chiefly translates shalom.24
  • Love (ἀγάπη, agapē) picks up “having been loved” from v. 1. It will appear again in a surprising place, the community’s “love-feasts” (v. 12), and once more in the command “keep yourselves in the love of God” (v. 21).

The three gifts are not three separate packages. Together they describe the life of the called community: God’s mercy received, God’s peace enjoyed, God’s love shared.

  1. Abbott-Smith, s.v. ἔλεος: “[in LXX chiefly for חֶסֶד] mercy, pity, compassion”; the entry lists Jude 2 among the benedictions and Jude 21 as mercy “of Christ.”↩︎

  2. Abbott-Smith, s.v. εἰρήνη: “[in LXX chiefly for שָׁלוֹם] peace.”↩︎

“be multiplied to you!”

The verb πληθυνθείη (plēthyntheiē) is an optative, the Greek mood used for wishes and prayers. It is rare in the New Testament, which has only about sixty-eight optative forms in all.25 Jude uses it twice: here and in Michael’s “The LORD rebuke you!” (v. 9).

The wish formula has a history. In the Greek translation of Daniel attributed to Theodotion, the royal letters of Nebuchadnezzar and Darius both open with εἰρήνη ὑμῖν πληθυνθείη, ‘peace be multiplied to you’ (Dan 4:1; 6:26).26 First and Second Peter use the same formula: “Grace and peace be multiplied to you!” (1 Pet 1:2, LSV; cf. 2 Pet 1:2). Jude uses a familiar form of greeting, apparently at home in Jewish letters, and fills it with his own triad.

The point of “multiplied” is abundance. Jude does not wish his readers a modest share of mercy, peace, and love; he prays that these will grow and overflow. Given the troubles the letter describes, that prayer is not decoration but provision. A community facing division needs more love, not less, and mercy that grows enough to reach those who are wavering (vv. 22–23).

  1. Computed from the MorphGNT/SBLGNT data: 68 verb forms in the optative mood in the whole New Testament.↩︎

  2. Greek Daniel (Theodotion) 4:1 and 6:26, in Brenton’s Greek Septuagint (eBible.org). Abbott-Smith, s.v. πληθύνω, also notes the construction with the dative of person in Daniel.↩︎

D. Original-Language Investigation

Greek Transliteration Basic meaning In Jude 1–2 Notes and cautions
Ἰούδας Ioudas Judah/Judas (personal name) The author Same name as Judas Iscariot; “Jude” is an English convention.
δοῦλος doulos slave, bondservant “servant of Jesus Christ” Implies belonging and obedience; also an OT honorific (“servant of the LORD”). “Servant” can obscure the ownership language; “slave” can obscure the honor. Both senses are present.
ἀδελφός adelphos brother; kinsman “brother of James” Can mean full brother, half-brother, or relative; see the Historical Introduction’s deep dive.
κλητός klētos called, invited “those called” Placed last for emphasis. In the NT “always of the Divine call” (Abbott-Smith).
ἠγαπημένοις (from ἀγαπάω) ēgapēmenois having been loved “having been loved in God [the] Father” Perfect passive participle: a settled state. Variant ἡγιασμένοις, “sanctified.”
τετηρημένοις (from τηρέω) tetērēmenois having been kept “having been kept in Jesus Christ” Perfect passive participle. Dative “Jesus Christ” may mean for, by, or in.
ἔλεος eleos mercy, compassion “mercy” In LXX chiefly for Hebrew ḥesed.
εἰρήνη eirēnē peace “peace” In LXX chiefly for shalom: wholeness, security.
ἀγάπη agapē love “love” See caution below.
πληθυνθείη (from πληθύνω) plēthyntheiē may it be multiplied “be multiplied” Aorist passive optative: a wish or prayer.

A caution about agapē. Popular teaching often claims that agapē and the verb agapaō name a uniquely divine, self-giving love, distinct from human affection. The New Testament’s own usage does not support so sharp a distinction. The same verb describes Demas, who deserted Paul, “having loved the present age” (2 Tim 4:10, LSV), and those of whom John says “men loved the darkness rather than the light” (John 3:19, LSV). Abbott-Smith’s lexicon notes that the traditional contrast between agapaō and phileō rests partly on a disputed etymology.27 What gives Christian agapē its distinctive character is not the word itself but its object and source: the love of God shown in Christ. Jude’s usage fits this. The readers are loved by God, and they are to keep themselves in God’s love.

A grammatical note on word order. Greek allows an author to wrap a noun in its descriptions. Jude’s “to the … called” encloses “in God the Father loved and for Jesus Christ kept.” The arrangement places God the Father and Jesus Christ in parallel as the sources of the readers’ security, and it saves the noun of identity for last.

  1. Abbott-Smith, s.v. ἀγαπάω, notes that the distinction from φιλέω is drawn “from its supposed etymology” and is qualified (“If this distinction holds…”).↩︎

E. The Historical and Cultural World

Letter-writing conventions. Thousands of letters survive from the Greco-Roman world, many of them on papyrus from Egypt. The typical opening was brief: “A to B, greetings (χαίρειν, chairein).” Jewish letters often used “peace” instead. Christian letter-writers took over the basic form and transformed it, replacing chairein with charis (“grace”) or with theological triads like Jude’s, and expanding the descriptions of sender and recipients to make theological claims. Jude’s opening is shorter than Paul’s, but it is just as deliberate.

Slavery in the Roman world. Slavery was pervasive in the first-century Mediterranean world. Slaves worked in households, farms, mines, workshops, and government offices. Some held positions of considerable responsibility as stewards and agents of their masters, and a slave of a powerful master could share some of that master’s status. Calling oneself “a slave of Jesus Christ” therefore said two things at once: complete submission, and representation of a master whose authority exceeded every other. Modern readers should hold both senses without romanticizing an institution that was, for most of those caught in it, brutal.

The family of Jesus. The household of Joseph and Mary in Nazareth was a family of craftsmen (Mark 6:3 calls Jesus “the carpenter”). Hegesippus’s account of Jude’s grandsons farming a small plot of land suggests a family of modest means in the following generations.28 By the time Jude wrote, members of this family were known throughout the churches. James led the church in Jerusalem, and “the brothers of the LORD” traveled as missionaries (1 Cor 9:5, LSV). Jude writes from inside this network of relationships, but he presents himself first of all as Christ’s servant.

  1. Eusebius, Ecclesiastical History 3.20.1–5, quoting Hegesippus.↩︎

F. Connections Across Scripture

Called and kept (Isa 41–42; Rom 8). The pairing of God’s call with God’s keeping goes back to Isaiah’s portrait of the servant Israel: “I have called you, / And I say to you, You [are] My servant, / I have chosen you, and not rejected you” (Isa 41:9, LSV); “I, YHWH, called you in righteousness, / And I lay hold on your hand, and keep you” (Isa 42:6, LSV). Paul’s chain of God’s actions in Rom 8:30, “whom He called, these also He declared righteous; and whom He declared righteous, these also He glorified” (LSV), expresses the same confidence that God finishes what he begins. Jude belongs to this stream: those God calls, he holds. This is a shared theme rather than a direct allusion.

Loved by God (Deut 7:7–8; Rom 1:7; 2 Thess 2:13). Israel’s election rested on God’s love, not on its merit (Deut 7:7–8). Paul addresses Roman believers as “beloved of God, called holy ones” (Rom 1:7, LSV), joining “loved” and “called” as Jude does. Second Thessalonians gives thanks for “brothers, beloved by the LORD, that God chose you from the beginning to salvation” (2 Thess 2:13, LSV).

Kept (John 17; 1 Pet 1:4–5; Rev 3:10). Jesus’ prayer, “Holy Father, keep them in Your Name” (John 17:11, LSV), uses the same verb as Jude, τηρέω. So does Jesus’ further petition that the Father “keep them out of the evil” (John 17:15, LSV), and his promise in Revelation: “I also will keep you from the hour of the trial” (Rev 3:10, LSV). Peter describes believers as people “who, in the power of God are being guarded, through faith, to salvation” (1 Pet 1:5, LSV). The Aaronic blessing, “YHWH bless you and keep you” (Num 6:24, LSV), stands behind all of this language. These passages share Jude’s conviction, though Jude’s wording is not drawn directly from any of them.

The greeting formula (Dan 4:1; 6:26; 1 Pet 1:2; 2 Pet 1:2). As noted above, “be multiplied” appears in royal letters in Greek Daniel and in the openings of 1 and 2 Peter. This is a shared epistolary convention rather than a literary dependence.

Mercy in benedictions (Gal 6:16; 1 Tim 1:2; 2 Tim 1:2; 2 John 3). “Mercy” appears in several New Testament blessings, but only Jude combines it with peace and love.

G. Scholarly and Theological Questions

1. Who is Jude? This question is treated in the Historical Introduction. For the opening verses, it is enough to note that the self-description “servant of Jesus Christ, and brother of James” is most naturally read as coming from the brother of Jesus and of James of Jerusalem. Scholars who regard the letter as pseudonymous read it as a later writer’s appeal to that family’s authority.

2. “Loved” or “sanctified”? The critical editions and the LSV read “loved.” The Byzantine tradition reads “sanctified.” The meaning differs only slightly, since both describe God’s action on behalf of believers. The choice affects how strongly the opening anticipates the letter’s theme of love (vv. 2, 12, 21).

3. “Kept for,” “by,” or “in” Jesus Christ? All three are grammatically possible. The choice affects the emphasis: Christ’s return, Christ’s agency, or union with Christ.

4. How does God’s keeping relate to human responsibility? Jude says that believers have been kept (v. 1). He urges them to keep themselves (v. 21). He closes by praising the God who is able to guard them (v. 24). Christian traditions have related these statements in different ways:

  • The Reformed tradition reads v. 1 and v. 24 as teaching the perseverance of the saints: those truly called and loved by God will be preserved to the end. On this view the command of v. 21 is the means by which God’s keeping is worked out.
  • Wesleyan and Arminian traditions emphasize that v. 21 is a real command whose neglect has real consequences, and they read the warnings of the letter (especially v. 5) as showing that those once delivered can fall away.
  • Catholic and Orthodox traditions speak of grace and human cooperation working together, with God’s keeping as the ground and enabling power of the believer’s perseverance.

Jude himself does not resolve the theological puzzle. He states both sides with equal force and lets them stand together. This book follows his lead. The keeping of God comes first and frames everything, and the call to keep oneself is meant seriously. Readers will weigh the systematic question according to their traditions, but they should notice that Jude saw no contradiction in saying both.

H. The Passage Within Jude

The greeting is the letter’s foundation stone. It introduces three threads the rest of the letter will pull tight:

  • Keeping. The readers are “kept” (v. 1). The rebels are “kept” for judgment (vv. 6, 13). The readers must “keep” themselves (v. 21). God is able to “guard” them (v. 24).
  • Love. The readers are “loved” (v. 1). Love is wished upon them (v. 2). Their meals are “love-feasts” that intruders spoil (v. 12). They must stay in “the love of God” (v. 21).
  • Mercy. Mercy is wished upon them (v. 2). They wait for Christ’s mercy (v. 21). They are to show mercy to others (vv. 22–23).

These threads form a frame. The opening greeting (vv. 1–2) and the closing exhortation and doxology (vv. 20–25) share most of their key words: love, keep, mercy, Jesus Christ, God. Whatever darkness comes between, the readers are meant to read it from inside this frame.

I. Contemporary Understanding

The following reflections suggest ways the passage may inform Christian life today. They are applications, not claims about what Jude’s first readers were thinking.

Identity before instruction. Jude tells his readers who they are before he tells them what to do. Modern Christian teaching often reverses the order and leads with demands. Jude’s order suggests that believers may face hard truths and difficult tasks better when they begin from the security of being called, loved, and kept. For churches facing conflict, this ordering may itself be part of faithful leadership.

Authority without self-promotion. Jude had a family connection to Jesus that few could claim, yet he introduced himself as a servant and named his brother James instead of the Lord. Leaders in churches and Christian organizations today might ask how they present their credentials, and whether their self-descriptions point to themselves or to the one they serve.

Being kept in a world of loss. Many people experience faith as fragile, something they might lose through doubt, failure, or suffering. Jude’s perfect participles (“having been loved,” “having been kept”) describe a settled state grounded in God’s action. They do not deny the reality of struggle; the rest of the letter shows that Jude took struggle seriously. They do offer a place to stand.

Blessing as a practice. Jude’s greeting is a prayer: “mercy and peace and love be multiplied to you!” Christians often greet one another casually. Jude’s opening might encourage the practice of speaking blessing deliberately, in letters, messages, and gatherings, wishing others an abundance of God’s gifts rather than mere pleasantries.

J. Reflection and Discussion

  1. Jude describes his readers by what God has done for them (called, loved, kept) before he describes anything they must do. How might this order change the way you approach the challenges described later in the letter?
  2. Why do you think Jude called himself a “servant of Jesus Christ” rather than “brother of the Lord”? What would each description have communicated to his readers?
  3. The phrase “kept in [or for, or by] Jesus Christ” can be read three ways. Which reading seems most natural to you, and what difference does the choice make?
  4. Jude says believers have been kept (v. 1) and must keep themselves (v. 21). How do you hold these two statements together? Where have you seen people emphasize one at the expense of the other?
  5. Of the three gifts Jude wishes his readers (mercy, peace, love), which do you think your own community most needs “multiplied” right now, and why?
