Chapter 2. The Faith Entrusted Once for All (Jude 3–4)

A. The Text

3 Beloved, using all diligence to write to you concerning our common salvation, I had necessity to write to you, exhorting [you] to fight for the faith once delivered to the holy ones, 4 for there came in certain men stealthily, having been written beforehand to this judgment long ago, impious, perverting the grace of our God to licentiousness, and denying our only Master and Lord—Jesus Christ.

— Jude 3–4, LSV

B. Plain-English Orientation

These two verses give the reason for the letter. Jude had been eager to write to his friends about the salvation they shared. Something forced a change of plan. People had slipped into the community unnoticed, people whose judgment was already on record. They were taking God’s grace as permission for immoral living, and in doing so they were denying Jesus Christ, the only Master and Lord. So Jude writes urgently instead, appealing to his readers to contend for the faith that had been handed over to God’s people once for all.

Verse 3 states the purpose of the letter: an appeal to contend for the faith. Verse 4 states the occasion, the intruders. Everything that follows explains and supports these two verses. Verses 5–19 show why the intruders deserve the judgment already “written beforehand,” and verses 20–23 show what contending for the faith actually looks like.

C. Phrase-by-Phrase Exploration

“Beloved”

The address ἀγαπητοί (agapētoi) echoes “having been loved” in v. 1 and the wish for “love” in v. 2. It is affectionate, and it is also strategic: Jude uses it three times (vv. 3, 17, 20), each at a point where he turns directly to the readers. Before he tells them about the intruders, he reminds them where they stand. They are beloved by God and by him.

“using all diligence to write to you concerning our common salvation”

The Greek phrase πᾶσαν σπουδὴν ποιούμενος (pasan spoudēn poioumenos) means “making every effort” or “being very eager.” The word σπουδή (spoudē) means haste, diligence, or earnestness. Jude had been fully intent on writing a letter of a different kind, and perhaps had already begun one.

Its subject was to be “our common salvation” (περὶ τῆς κοινῆς ἡμῶν σωτηρίας). The adjective κοινός (koinos) means “shared, held in common.” It is the word Luke uses of the first believers who “had all things common” (Acts 2:44, LSV), and Paul calls Titus his “true child according to a common faith” (Titus 1:4, LSV). It does not mean “ordinary.” The salvation Jude wanted to celebrate belongs equally to him and his readers, and perhaps to Jewish and Gentile believers alike. It is the common possession of the whole people of God.

We do not know what that other letter would have contained, or whether Jude ever wrote it. Some readers wish he had. Yet the letter we have is not a departure from the subject of “our common salvation.” It is a defense of it. The salvation he wanted to celebrate is the very thing the intruders threaten.

“I had necessity to write to you”

The change of plan is expressed with strong language: ἀνάγκην ἔσχον (anankēn eschon), “I had necessity,” or “I found it necessary,” “I felt compelled.” Paul uses the same noun when he says that “necessity is laid on me” to proclaim the good news (1 Cor 9:16, LSV). Something had happened that left Jude no choice.

There is also a quiet shift in the verb. The first “to write” (γράφειν, graphein) is in the present tense, describing the ongoing project he had in mind. The second (γράψαι, grapsai) is aorist, describing this particular letter as a single urgent act. The grammar fits the story: a long-planned general letter, interrupted by an immediate and specific one.

“exhorting [you]”

The participle παρακαλῶν (parakalōn) means “urging, appealing, exhorting.” It is a warm and pastoral word, from the same family as παράκλητος (paraklētos), the title of the Holy Spirit that the LSV renders “Comforter” (John 14:16), and that other versions render ‘Helper’ or ‘Advocate’. Jude does not command his readers like a superior issuing orders. He appeals to them as a brother. The LSV supplies “[you]” because the Greek leaves the object to be understood.

“to fight for the faith”

Here is the heart of the appeal. The verb ἐπαγωνίζεσθαι (epagōnizesthai) occurs nowhere else in the New Testament. It belongs to the family of ἀγών (agōn), the word for an athletic contest or struggle, from which English gets “agony.” Paul uses related words for the Christian life: “I have striven the good strife” (2 Tim 4:7, LSV); “Be striving the good strife of the faith” (1 Tim 6:12, LSV). The prefix ἐπ- (ep-) may add a sense of “for” or “on behalf of,” and the dative case of “the faith” means “contend for the faith.” The faith is what the contest defends, not the weapon used in it.

The LSV’s “fight” is vigorous and literal. Other translations have “contend earnestly” (WEB, ASV, KJV) or “contend” (BSB). The image is of exertion, of a struggle that costs effort. It is not an image of violence. The rest of the letter will show what this struggle involves. The intruders are not to be attacked; they are to be recognized for what they are and resisted. The readers’ main task, spelled out in vv. 20–23, is to build themselves up in the faith, to pray, to keep themselves in God’s love, and to show mercy to those who waver. Jude’s contending is defined by his closing instructions, not by the heat of his warnings.

“once delivered to the holy ones”

Three words in this phrase carry great weight.

“Once” translates ἅπαξ (hapax), “once, once for all.” The same word describes Christ’s sacrifice: “now He has been revealed once, at the full end of the ages, for [the] annulling of sin through His sacrifice” (Heb 9:26, LSV); “Christ suffered once for sin—righteous for unrighteous—that He might lead us to God” (1 Pet 3:18, LSV). The faith was handed over on a particular occasion, completely. It does not need supplementing by later revelations, and that is exactly the claim the intruders, with their ‘dreams’ (v. 8), appear to have made.

“Delivered” translates παραδοθείσῃ (paradotheisē), from παραδίδωμι (paradidōmi), “to hand over, pass on.” This is the technical language of tradition. Paul uses it for the most important things he taught: “For I delivered to you as most important what I also received: that Christ died for our sins according to the Writings” (1 Cor 15:3, LSV), and “For I received from the LORD that which I also delivered to you, that the Lord Jesus in the night in which He was delivered up, took bread” (1 Cor 11:23, LSV). Jewish teachers used the same pair of ideas, receiving and handing on, for the transmission of sacred teaching. The Mishnah, compiled around AD 200, opens its tractate Avot with a chain of tradition from Moses through Joshua and the elders. Christianity understood itself from the beginning as a message received and passed on through faithful witnesses.

The aorist passive participle presents the delivering as a completed act, with God as the implied giver. The faith did not originate with the church; it was given to the church.

“The holy ones” translates τοῖς ἁγίοις (tois hagiois), “the saints.” The LSV’s “holy ones” helps modern readers avoid thinking of a special class of exceptionally virtuous Christians. In the Bible “the holy ones” are God’s people as a whole, set apart for him. Daniel foresees that “the saints of the Most High receive the kingdom” (Dan 7:18, LSV), where the Aramaic word behind “saints” means “holy ones.” The faith was entrusted to the whole community, not to a spiritual elite. That matters, because the intruders, as v. 19 will suggest, seem to have divided the community into ranks.

“for there came in certain men stealthily”

Verse 4 explains why the appeal was necessary. The verb παρεισέδυσαν (pareisedysan) is another word found only here in the New Testament. It means “to slip in secretly, steal in.”29 It pictures someone entering through a side door, unnoticed. Paul uses related language of the “false brothers brought in unaware, who came in secretly to spy out our liberty” (Gal 2:4, LSV), and 2 Peter of false teachers “who will stealthily bring in destructive sects” (2 Pet 2:1, LSV).

The point is not necessarily that the intruders hid their identity. It is that their arrival was not recognized for what it was. They came as fellow believers, perhaps as traveling teachers welcomed according to the customs of Christian hospitality, and only later did their influence become clear. The phrase “certain men” (τινες ἄνθρωποι, tines anthrōpoi) is deliberately vague and somewhat dismissive. Jude never names them.

  1. Abbott-Smith, s.v. παρεισδύω: “to slip in secretly, steal in,” noting the form παρεισεδύησαν read by some editions.↩︎

“having been written beforehand to this judgment long ago”

This is one of the most debated phrases in the letter. The Greek οἱ πάλαι προγεγραμμένοι εἰς τοῦτο τὸ κρίμα (hoi palai progegrammenoi eis touto to krima) means literally “those long ago written-beforehand for this judgment.”

  • προγράφω (prographō) means “to write before” or “to write publicly.”30 Paul uses it for the scriptures: “as many things as were written before, for our instruction were written before” (Rom 15:4, LSV).
  • πάλαι (palai) means “long ago, of old.”31
  • κρίμα (krima) means “judgment” or “condemnation,” the verdict and its sentence.

What does it mean that these people were “written beforehand” for judgment? Three main interpretations have been offered.

  1. Written about in earlier prophecies. The intruders’ judgment was described in advance in the scriptures and prophecies Jude is about to cite: the wilderness generation, the angels, Sodom, Cain, Balaam, Korah, and especially Enoch’s prophecy against the “impious.” “This judgment” points forward to the judgments Jude will describe. On this reading, Jude is saying: people like these were written about long ago, and what was written about them is about to be read out. The word “long ago” (πάλαι) fits a prophecy as ancient as Enoch’s. This is the interpretation most modern commentators favor, and the structure of the letter supports it.
  2. Written in heavenly books. Jewish writings of the period speak of heavenly records in which deeds and destinies are inscribed (for example, the “heavenly tablets” of 1 Enoch 81 and 93:2). Jude might mean that the intruders’ condemnation was already recorded in heaven.
  3. Predestined to condemnation. Some theological traditions have read the phrase as a statement of individual reprobation, a divine decree that these persons would be condemned. Clement of Alexandria already addressed this, insisting that the intruders were ordained to judgment “not that they might become impious, but that, being now impious, they were ordained to judgment.”32

The first interpretation best fits the flow of the letter, which immediately sets out “written” examples of judgment. This book adopts it while acknowledging the others. The phrase is a statement about the certainty of judgment on a certain kind of behavior, not a statement that particular persons were created for damnation. It is also, in context, a word of reassurance. The intruders’ arrival had not taken God by surprise.

  1. Abbott-Smith, s.v. προγράφω: “to write before: Ro 15:4, Eph 3:3, Ju 4. to write in public, placard, proclaim … Ga 3:1.”↩︎

  2. Abbott-Smith, s.v. πάλαι: “long ago, of old, in time past.”↩︎

  3. Clement of Alexandria, Adumbrationes on Jude 4, ANF 2 (https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0211.htm).↩︎

“impious”

The single word ἀσεβεῖς (asebeis), “ungodly, impious,” stands alone in the Greek like a verdict. It is Jude’s key term for the intruders. The noun, verb, and adjective of this word family occur six times in the letter, four of them in the quotation from Enoch: “to convict all their impious ones, concerning all their works of impiety that they did impiously, and concerning all the harsh [words] that impious sinners spoke” (v. 15). By calling the intruders “impious” here, Jude labels them with the very word of the prophecy he will quote. The asebeia family describes a failure of reverence toward God, a life lived as though God were not to be feared.

“perverting the grace of our God to licentiousness”

This phrase names the intruders’ central error. The participle μετατιθέντες (metatithentes), from μετατίθημι (metatithēmi), means “to transfer, change,” and here “to make one thing a pretext for another.”33 The intruders take “the grace of our God” and turn it into licentiousness, ἀσέλγεια (aselgeia), a word for unrestrained, shameless behavior, often sexual.34 It appears in the New Testament’s vice lists: “adultery, whoredom, uncleanness, licentiousness” (Gal 5:19, LSV).

The error is ancient and recurring. Paul had to answer it: “Will we continue in sin that grace may abound? … Let it not be!” (Rom 6:1–2, LSV); “you were called to freedom, brothers, only the freedom [is] not for an occasion to the flesh” (Gal 5:13, LSV). Peter warns believers not to have “freedom as the cloak of evil” (1 Pet 2:16, LSV). Titus offers the positive counterpart: “the saving grace of God has appeared to all men, teaching us, that denying the impiety and the worldly desires, we may live soberly, and righteously, and piously in the present age” (Titus 2:11–12, LSV). In Titus, grace teaches the denial of impiety. In Jude, the impious deny the Master and use grace as license.

The intruders probably did not see themselves as opponents of grace. They likely saw themselves as its most consistent champions: if God forgives freely, why should conduct matter? Jude’s verdict is that such a view does not exalt grace; it perverts it.

  1. Abbott-Smith, s.v. μετατίθημι: “to change … fig., i.e. to make one thing a pretext for another, χάριν εἰς ἀσέλγειαν, Ju 4.”↩︎

  2. Abbott-Smith, s.v. ἀσέλγεια: “licentiousness, wantonness, excess.”↩︎

“and denying our only Master and Lord—Jesus Christ”

The participle ἀρνούμενοι (arnoumenoi), “denying,” does not necessarily mean the intruders publicly renounced Christ in words. The New Testament often speaks of denial by conduct: “They profess to know God, but they deny [Him] by their works” (Titus 1:16, LSV). Since the intruders were inside the church, it is likely that they confessed Jesus with their lips while denying his lordship with their lives. To use grace as license is to refuse the authority of the one who gave it.

The titles are striking. “Master” translates δεσπότην (despotēn), the accusative of δεσπότης (despotēs), the word for an owner of slaves and a ruler with absolute authority.35 In the Septuagint and elsewhere in the New Testament it is used of God (“LORD, You [are] God, who made the heaven, and the earth,” Acts 4:24, LSV; cf. Luke 2:29). Second Peter uses it of Christ, “denying the Master who bought them” (2 Pet 2:1, LSV). “Only” (μόνος, monos) is the language of Jewish monotheism. In v. 25, Jude will praise “the only … God our Savior.” To call Jesus “our only Master and Lord” is to apply to him language that belongs to the God of Israel. Jude, who calls himself Jesus’ doulos in v. 1, now calls him the despotēs of all believers.

The LSV’s dash (“our only Master and Lord—Jesus Christ”) makes clear that both titles refer to Jesus. In Greek this is signaled by a single article (τόν, ton) governing both nouns, a construction in which two singular personal nouns joined by “and” under one article normally refer to the same person.

A textual note. The Byzantine tradition and the Textus Receptus add θεόν (theon), “God”: “the only Master God and our Lord Jesus Christ.” The King James Version translated this as two persons: “denying the only Lord God, and our Lord Jesus Christ.” The WEB, translating the same Majority Text, renders it as one person: “denying our only Master, God, and Lord, Jesus Christ.” The critical editions omit θεόν, and the LSV follows them. On either reading, Jude’s Christology is high.

  1. Abbott-Smith, s.v. δεσπότης: “a master, lord, correlative of δοῦλος”; used “as title of God” and “of Christ, II Pe 2:1, Ju 4.” Abbott-Smith quotes Thayer that δεσπότης “denoted absolute ownership and uncontrolled power.”↩︎

D. Original-Language Investigation

Greek Transliteration Basic meaning In context Notes
σπουδή spoudē haste, diligence, eagerness “using all diligence” Eager intent, not anxiety.
κοινός koinos common, shared “our common salvation” “Shared,” not “ordinary”; cf. Titus 1:4.
ἀνάγκη anankē necessity, compulsion “I had necessity” Strong external pressure; cf. 1 Cor 9:16.
παρακαλέω parakaleō urge, appeal, encourage “exhorting” Warm pastoral appeal.
ἐπαγωνίζομαι epagōnizomai contend for “to fight for” NT hapax; athletic/agonistic imagery; dative = “for” the faith. Do not overread the prefix.
πίστις pistis faith, trust; the faith “the faith” Here the content believed (as in Gal 1:23), not the act of believing.
ἅπαξ hapax once, once for all “once” Finality and completeness (Heb 9:26; 1 Pet 3:18).
παραδίδωμι paradidōmi hand over, deliver “delivered” Technical term for transmitting tradition (1 Cor 11:2, 23; 15:3).
ἅγιοι hagioi holy ones, saints “the holy ones” All God’s people.
παρεισδύω pareisdyō slip in secretly “came in … stealthily” NT hapax. Some manuscripts (followed by WH) have the variant aorist form παρεισεδύησαν.
προγράφω prographō write beforehand; placard “written beforehand” Rom 15:4 (“written before”); Gal 3:1 (“publicly portrayed”).
πάλαι palai long ago “long ago” Absolute past.
κρίμα krima judgment, sentence “this judgment” Points forward to vv. 5–15.
ἀσεβής asebēs impious, ungodly “impious” Jude’s key word (×6 in the word family).
χάρις charis grace, favor “the grace of our God” SBLGNT reads the rarer accusative form χάριτα.
μετατίθημι metatithēmi transfer, change “perverting” To turn one thing into a pretext for another.
ἀσέλγεια aselgeia licentiousness, debauchery “licentiousness” Shameless excess, often sexual.
δεσπότης despotēs master, owner, sovereign “Master” Of God in LXX/NT; here of Christ.
ἀρνέομαι arneomai deny, disown, renounce “denying” Can be denial by conduct (Titus 1:16).

Two cautions.

  1. Etymology and ἐπαγωνίζομαι. Because the word is related to agōn, sermons sometimes describe Jude’s readers as athletes “agonizing” in the arena. The word does belong to the language of contest, and the image is fair as far as it goes. But the meaning of a rare compound is best derived from its context, not from a picture assembled from its parts. Here the context says only that the readers should exert themselves on behalf of the faith. Jude defines how in vv. 20–23.
  2. “The faith” vs. “faith.” With the article and with “once delivered,” pistis here means the body of belief, not personal trust. That is not a sign of a “later” Christianity: Paul already speaks this way in Gal 1:23. Equally, Jude does not imagine faith as a mere list of propositions. The faith is “most holy” (v. 20), something believers build themselves up on.

E. The Historical and Cultural World

Traveling teachers and Christian hospitality. The early churches depended on traveling apostles, prophets, and teachers, and they extended them hospitality. That openness made communities vulnerable. The Didache, an early Christian manual of church practice usually dated between the late first and early second century, gives practical tests for visitors. “Whosoever … comes and teaches you all these things that have been said before, receive him. But if the teacher himself turn and teach another doctrine to the destruction of this, hear him not.” An apostle should stay no more than a day or two, and “if he ask money, he is a false prophet.” “Not every one that speaks in the Spirit is a prophet; but only if he hold the ways of the Lord. Therefore from their ways shall the false prophet and the prophet be known.”36 These instructions match Jude’s concerns closely: teaching measured against what was handed down, character as the test of a claimed spiritual gift, and suspicion of teachers who seek profit (vv. 11, 16). The Didache was not written to Jude’s readers, and the two documents are independent. It does show that the problem Jude faced was a recognized one in the early churches.

Contests and the agōn. Athletic games were a major feature of Greco-Roman civic life, and the language of contest (agōn, “struggle, competition”) was widely used metaphorically by philosophers and moralists for the moral life. Hellenistic Jewish writers also used it of faithfulness under persecution. Jude’s word draws on this widely understood world of strenuous, disciplined effort.

Tradition in Judaism and early Christianity. Jewish teachers carefully transmitted the interpretation of the Law from teacher to student. Early Christians, many of them Jews, used the same vocabulary of “receiving” and “handing on” for the gospel and for the teachings of Jesus. When Jude speaks of “the faith once delivered,” he speaks the language of a community conscious of being entrusted with something it did not invent.

  1. Didache 11.1–2, 4–6, 8; 12.1–5, trans. M. B. Riddle, ANF 7 (https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0714.htm).↩︎

F. Connections Across Scripture

Contending for the faith (Phil 1:27; 1 Tim 6:12; 2 Tim 4:7). Paul urges the Philippians to stand “with one soul, striving together for the faith of the good news” (Phil 1:27, LSV), using a related compound (συναθλέω, “strive together”). Timothy is told, “Be striving the good strife of the faith” (1 Tim 6:12, LSV), and Paul says of himself, “I have striven the good strife, I have finished the course, I have kept the faith” (2 Tim 4:7, LSV). These are shared themes and vocabulary rather than quotations. Together they show that “contending for the faith” was part of the common moral language of early Christianity.

Guarding a deposit (1 Tim 6:20; 2 Tim 1:14). Paul tells Timothy, “guard the thing entrusted” (1 Tim 6:20, LSV) and “guard the good thing committed through the Holy Spirit that is dwelling in us” (2 Tim 1:14, LSV). The image of a deposit held in trust parallels Jude’s faith “once delivered.”

Received and delivered (1 Cor 11:23; 15:1–3). The closest verbal parallels are Paul’s statements of tradition. Note especially 1 Cor 15:1–2: “the good news that I proclaimed to you, which you also received, in which you also have stood, through which you are also being saved” (LSV). The gospel is received, stood in, and saving, like Jude’s “common salvation.”

Infiltrators (Gal 2:4; 2 Pet 2:1). Paul’s “false brothers brought in unaware, who came in secretly” (Gal 2:4, LSV) offers the nearest Pauline parallel to Jude’s “came in … stealthily.” The parallel with 2 Pet 2:1 (“stealthily bring in destructive sects, even denying the Master who bought them”) is one of the strongest verbal links between Jude and 2 Peter (see Appendix C).

Grace and license (Rom 6:1–2, 15; Gal 5:13; 1 Pet 2:16; Titus 2:11–12). Discussed above. These passages show that the abuse of grace was a danger the apostles recognized and resisted in several places.

Denial by conduct (Titus 1:16; Matt 10:33; 2 Tim 2:12). Jesus warned, “whoever will deny Me before men, I also will deny him before My Father who is in the heavens” (Matt 10:33, LSV). Jude’s charge stands in this line.

G. Scholarly and Theological Questions

1. Did Jude write the other letter? Some have suggested that the planned letter about “our common salvation” might survive elsewhere, for example as 2 Peter. There is no evidence for this, and most scholars regard the question as unanswerable.

2. Is “the faith once delivered” a sign of late authorship? As noted in the Historical Introduction, some scholars see in “the faith” a fixed body of doctrine typical of later “early catholic” Christianity. Others point out that Paul spoke of “the faith” as content (Gal 1:23) and of delivering traditions (1 Cor 11:2, 23; 15:3) in the 50s. The phrase is compatible with an early date.

3. What exactly is “the faith”? Jude does not define it. From the letter itself we can infer that it includes the lordship of Jesus Christ (v. 4), the reality of judgment (vv. 5–15), the grace of God that does not license immorality (v. 4), the apostles’ teaching (v. 17), the work of the Holy Spirit (vv. 19–20), the love of God and the mercy of Christ (v. 21), and the hope of eternal life (v. 21). That is, it includes both belief and the shape of life that belief requires. Christian traditions differ on where this “faith once delivered” is now authoritatively found. Many Protestants locate it in Scripture alone. Catholic and Orthodox Christians locate it in Scripture together with the church’s living tradition. All agree that it was given, not invented.

4. What does “written beforehand” imply about predestination? See the discussion in section C above. The phrase has been read in support of divine reprobation, but the context points most naturally to recorded prophecies of judgment, which Jude proceeds to cite.

5. Does “only Master” imply that Jesus is God? Jude does not use the word “God” of Jesus (unless the Byzantine θεόν is original). He does apply to Jesus titles and monotheistic language (“only Master,” “Lord”) that Jewish usage reserved for God. If the reading “Jesus” in v. 5 is original (see Chapter 3), Jude also attributes to Jesus the exodus deliverance, the defining saving act of Israel’s God. Many scholars see Jude as evidence of a very early and very high Christology emerging within Jewish Christianity.

H. The Passage Within Jude

Verses 3 and 4 are the hinge on which the letter turns. Verse 3 states the appeal: contend for the faith. Verse 4 states the reason: intruders have come in. The rest of the letter unfolds both:

  • Verses 5–19 expand v. 4. They show from scripture, tradition, and prophecy that the intruders’ judgment was indeed “written beforehand,” and they describe the intruders’ “impious” character in detail.
  • Verses 20–23 expand v. 3. They show what “contending for the faith” means in practice: building up, praying, keeping, waiting, and showing mercy.

Jude’s frequent use of “impious” (vv. 4, 15, 18) and the word “faith” at both ends of this structure (vv. 3, 20) mark these connections.

I. Contemporary Understanding

These are suggested applications, distinct from the text’s original meaning.

Contending without contention. Jude’s call to “fight for the faith” has often been used to justify combativeness, sometimes over matters far removed from the core of the gospel. Jude’s own letter shows a different picture. His concern was the lordship of Christ and the moral meaning of grace, not secondary disputes. His instructions for contending (vv. 20–23) are about spiritual formation and mercy, not argument and exclusion. Christians today might ask whether their “contending” looks like Jude’s: urgent about what matters most, and practiced through prayer, love, and rescue.

The faith as gift and trust. “Once delivered to the holy ones” means the faith belongs to the whole people of God and was entrusted, not invented. For modern believers this may be both humbling and freeing. They do not need to create a faith that fits their moment, and they are responsible for passing on what they received.

Grace that teaches. Every generation meets the temptation to treat grace as permission. Jude may help believers see that grace and holiness are not rivals. The grace of God that forgives is the same grace that “teaches” a transformed life (Titus 2:11–12).

Recognizing infiltration without paranoia. Jude’s intruders “came in … stealthily,” and their influence was recognized only over time. Churches today might learn from the early Christian practice of testing teaching by its fidelity to the received faith and testing teachers by their conduct, as the Didache advised. They should do this without turning community life into suspicion of every newcomer. Jude’s own ending (vv. 22–23) shows that vigilance and mercy belong together.

J. Reflection and Discussion

  1. Jude intended to write about “our common salvation” but felt compelled to write a warning instead. Have you experienced a situation in which encouragement had to give way to correction? How did you decide?
  2. What do you think belongs to “the faith once delivered to the holy ones,” and what is secondary? How might different Christian traditions answer this question?
  3. The intruders likely saw themselves as champions of grace. How can a community distinguish between a genuine emphasis on grace and the “perverting” of grace that Jude describes?
  4. Jude says the intruders denied Jesus as Master and Lord, probably by their conduct rather than their words. In what ways can people confess Jesus with their lips while denying him with their lives?
  5. If contending for the faith is defined by vv. 20–23, what would it look like for you or your community to “fight for the faith” this week?

K. Sources

  • Lexical data: Abbott-Smith, Manual Greek Lexicon, s.vv. παρεισδύω, προγράφω, πάλαι, μετατίθημι, ἀσέλγεια, δεσπότης, ἐπαγωνίζομαι, ἅπαξ.
  • Didache 11–12, trans. M. B. Riddle, ANF 7.
  • Clement of Alexandria, Adumbrationes on Jude, ANF 2.
  • Interpretation of “written beforehand” as recorded prophecy: Bauckham, Jude, 2 Peter, on v. 4 (cited from prior knowledge).

Chapter 3. Three Ancient Warnings: The Exodus, the Angels, and Sodom (Jude 5–7)

A. The Text

5 Now I intend to remind you, you once having known all this, that Jesus, having saved a people out of the land of Egypt, He secondly destroyed those not having believed; 6 messengers also, those who did not keep their own principality, but left their proper dwelling, He has kept in eternal bonds under darkness until [the] judgment of [the] great day, 7 as Sodom and Gomorrah, and the cities around them, in like manner to these [messengers], having given themselves to whoredom, and having gone after other flesh, have been set before [as] an example, undergoing the justice of continuous fire.

— Jude 5–7, LSV

B. Plain-English Orientation

Jude now begins to show why the intruders’ judgment was “written beforehand” (v. 4). He reminds his readers of three well-known examples of God’s judgment:

  1. The wilderness generation. The people whom the Lord saved from Egypt were later destroyed when they refused to trust him (v. 5).
  2. The rebellious angels. Heavenly beings who abandoned their assigned place are now held in darkness until the final judgment (v. 6).
  3. Sodom and Gomorrah. The cities that pursued sexual immorality and “other flesh” now stand as a warning example of fiery judgment (v. 7).

Each example concerns people or beings who had a privileged position. Israel had been redeemed, the angels had a heavenly station, and Sodom and its neighbors lived in a fertile, well-watered land (Gen 13:10). Each abandoned that position through unbelief, rebellion, or immorality, and each came under judgment. The implication for the intruders, and for anyone tempted to follow them, is plain. Belonging to God’s people does not guarantee safety for those who turn grace into license.

Jude’s opening, “I intend to remind you,” matters too. He is not telling his readers anything new. He is calling to mind what they already know, in a way that makes its present relevance unmistakable.

C. Phrase-by-Phrase Exploration

“Now I intend to remind you, you once having known all this”

The verb ὑπομνῆσαι (hypomnēsai), “to remind,” places Jude in the role of a teacher who refreshes memory rather than introducing novelty. That role fits his claim that the faith was delivered “once” (v. 3). The readers already “know all this,” literally “having known all things once” (εἰδότας ὑμᾶς ἅπαξ πάντα, eidotas hymas hapax panta). The participle εἰδότας (eidotas), “knowing,” is concessive: although you know. Second Peter uses the same courtesy, “I will always be ready to remind you about these things, although you have known [them]” (2 Pet 1:12, LSV).

The word ἅπαξ (hapax), “once” or “once for all,” has wandered in the manuscripts. In some it stands with “knowing” (“you who once for all knew all things”). In others it moves into the next clause (“the Lord, having once saved a people”). The SBLGNT and NA28 place it with “knowing,” and the LSV follows them: “you once having known all this.” The echo of v. 3 is deliberate: the faith was delivered once for all, and the readers once for all came to know it. What remains is to remember.

“that Jesus, having saved a people out of the land of Egypt”

This is one of the most remarkable phrases in the New Testament, and one of the most debated. The LSV reads “Jesus.” Many other translations read “the Lord” (WEB, ASV, KJV), and the BSB reads “Jesus” with a note. The difference is not a translation choice. It reflects a difference in the Greek manuscripts, explored in the deep dive below.

If “Jesus” is original, Jude says that Jesus himself, before his human birth, was the one who delivered Israel from Egypt. That is a striking claim, but not an isolated one. Paul says that the Israelites in the wilderness “were drinking of a spiritual rock following them, and the rock was the Christ” (1 Cor 10:4, LSV), and he warns, “neither may we tempt the Christ, as also certain of them tempted” (1 Cor 10:9, LSV). John says that Isaiah “saw His glory, and spoke of Him” (John 12:41, LSV), referring to Jesus and to Isaiah’s vision of the LORD in the temple. Jude’s reading belongs to the same early Christian conviction that the one revealed in Jesus was already active in Israel’s history.

The verb σώσας (sōsas), “having saved,” is the ordinary New Testament word for salvation. Its use here recalls the language of the exodus itself: “YHWH saves Israel out of the hand of the Egyptians in that day” (Exod 14:30, LSV). The exodus was Israel’s defining experience of salvation. Jude’s point depends on this. The people who were later destroyed were not outsiders; they had been saved.

“He secondly destroyed those not having believed”

The phrase τὸ δεύτερον (to deuteron) literally means “the second time” or “secondly.” It can simply mean “afterward” (WEB: “afterward destroyed”), but its natural sense sets two acts side by side: first salvation, then destruction. The LSV keeps the literal “secondly.”

The reference is to the wilderness generation, which refused to enter the promised land after the report of the spies. God says to Moses, “Until when do they not believe in Me, for all the signs which I have done in its midst?” (Num 14:11, LSV). The sentence follows: “Your carcasses fall in this wilderness … who have murmured against Me” (Num 14:29, LSV). The aorist participle “those not having believed” (τοὺς μὴ πιστεύσαντας, tous mē pisteusantas) sums up the whole episode.

The warning in Jude’s first example is the most pointed of the three, because it concerns God’s own redeemed people. The intruders are inside the church. They share its meals and claim its grace. Jude’s readers might suppose that being among the saved is protection enough. The wilderness generation shows otherwise. The early church used this example often: Paul in 1 Corinthians 10 and the author of Hebrews (“we see that they were not able to enter in because of unbelief,” Heb 3:19, LSV). Jude uses it first in his series, perhaps for that reason.

“messengers also, those who did not keep their own principality”

The LSV’s “messengers” translates ἀγγέλους (angelous), the ordinary word for “messengers” that, in a heavenly context, means “angels.” The LSV renders it consistently as “messengers,” but the context makes clear that heavenly beings are meant.

These angels “did not keep” (μὴ τηρήσαντας, mē tērēsantas) “their own principality” (τὴν ἑαυτῶν ἀρχήν, tēn heautōn archēn). The word ἀρχή (archē) means “beginning,” “rule,” or “domain.” Here it most likely means the position of authority and the sphere of responsibility God had assigned them. The New Testament can call angelic powers “principalities” (ἀρχαί, archai; e.g., Rom 8:38; Eph 6:12). The LSV’s “principality” preserves that link. The WEB has “their first domain,” the ASV “their own principality,” and the BSB “their positions of authority.”

Note the first play on “keep.” The readers have been “kept” (v. 1). These angels did not “keep” what God had given them.

“but left their proper dwelling”

The angels also “left” or “abandoned” (ἀπολιπόντας, apolipontas) “their proper dwelling” (τὸ ἴδιον οἰκητήριον, to idion oikētērion). An οἰκητήριον is a habitation, a dwelling place.37 Paul uses the same rare word for the resurrection body: “our dwelling that is from Heaven” (2 Cor 5:2, LSV). The angels’ proper home was heaven. They left it.

Which angels, and what did they do? Jude assumes his readers know the story. As the deep dive below shows, they almost certainly did: it is the story of the “sons of God” who took human wives in Genesis 6:1–4, as expanded in the Jewish tradition preserved in 1 Enoch.

  1. Abbott-Smith, s.v. οἰκητήριον: “a habitation: Ju 6; trop., II Co 5:2.”↩︎

“He has kept in eternal bonds under darkness until [the] judgment of [the] great day”

Now comes the second, ironic “keep.” The angels who did not keep their position, God “has kept” (τετήρηκεν, tetērēken, perfect tense: has kept and still keeps) under guard. Jude’s wordplay states a principle: those who refuse to keep their God-given place are themselves kept, for judgment.

  • “Eternal bonds” (δεσμοῖς ἀϊδίοις, desmois aidiois). The adjective ἀΐδιος (aidios), “everlasting,” appears elsewhere in the New Testament only in Rom 1:20, of God’s “eternal power.” The “bonds” are chains of imprisonment.
  • “Under darkness” (ὑπὸ ζόφον, hypo zophon). The word ζόφος (zophos) means deep gloom. In Homer it was used of the gloom of the underworld.38 It appears again in v. 13 (“the gloom of the darkness”) and in the parallel passages of 2 Peter (2:4, 17).
  • “Until [the] judgment of [the] great day” (εἰς κρίσιν μεγάλης ἡμέρας, eis krisin megalēs hēmeras). The imprisonment is not the final penalty. It is custody awaiting trial. The “great day” is the final judgment, the “day of the LORD” of the prophets.

These phrases match the language of 1 Enoch closely, as the deep dive shows.

  1. Abbott-Smith, s.v. ζόφος: “in Hom. the gloom of the under-world; hence, darkness, deep gloom … He 12:18, II Pe 2:4, 17, Ju 6, 13.”↩︎

“as Sodom and Gomorrah, and the cities around them”

The third example, Σόδομα καὶ Γόμορρα (Sodoma kai Gomorra), is the most famous. Genesis 19 tells how God “rained on Sodom and on Gomorrah brimstone and fire from YHWH, from the heavens; and He overthrows these cities, and all the circuit” (Gen 19:24–25, LSV). The “cities around them” (αἱ περὶ αὐτὰς πόλεις, hai peri autas poleis) are Admah and Zeboim, named in Deut 29:23 and Hos 11:8 as destroyed with Sodom and Gomorrah. (Zoar, the fifth city of the plain, was spared at Lot’s request.)

“in like manner to these [messengers]”

The Greek τὸν ὅμοιον τρόπον τούτοις (ton homoion tropon toutois) means “in the same way as these.” Grammatically, “these” (τούτοις, toutois) is masculine, so it cannot refer to the cities (πόλεις, poleis), which is feminine. It most naturally refers to the nearest masculine plural, the angels of v. 6. The LSV makes this explicit by supplying “[messengers]” in brackets. (Some interpreters have suggested that “these” looks ahead to the intruders of v. 8, but the immediate sense is a comparison between Sodom and the angels.)

So the comparison is this: the cities sinned in the same way as the angels. In what way? The next phrases answer.

“having given themselves to whoredom”

The participle ἐκπορνεύσασαι (ekporneusasai) is a strengthened form of the common verb for sexual immorality, πορνεύω (porneuō). Its prefix ἐκ- (ek-) suggests going to excess, “giving oneself up to fornication.”39 The word occurs nowhere else in the New Testament. In the Septuagint it often describes Israel’s spiritual unfaithfulness, pictured as prostitution, but here, as the following phrase shows, the sense is literal.

  1. Abbott-Smith, s.v. ἐκπορνεύω: “strengthened form of πορνεύω, implying excessive indulgence; mid., to give oneself up to fornication: Ju 7.”↩︎

“and having gone after other flesh”

The phrase ἀπελθοῦσαι ὀπίσω σαρκὸς ἑτέρας (apelthousai opisō sarkos heteras) means literally “having gone away after other (or different) flesh.” The KJV’s famous “strange flesh” and the WEB’s “strange flesh” translate it. The BSB has “perversion.” The LSV keeps the literal “other flesh.”

What is “other flesh”? This phrase has become a flashpoint in modern debates about sexuality, and it needs careful handling. The deep dive below sets out the evidence. In brief, the parallel Jude has just drawn with the angels (“in like manner to these”) strongly suggests the following: as the angels crossed the boundary between heaven and earth to unite with human women, so the men of Sodom sought sexual relations with beings of a different order, namely the angels who visited Lot (Gen 19:1–5). On this reading, “other flesh” refers above all to the attempted violation of the angelic visitors. Jude’s emphasis falls on the transgression of the created order.

“have been set before [as] an example”

The verb πρόκεινται (prokeintai), “they are set forth, displayed,” is in the present tense: the cities are still set forth. The noun δεῖγμα (deigma), another New Testament hapax, means “a thing shown,” a specimen or example, and here a warning example.40 Second Peter uses the related ὑπόδειγμα (hypodeigma): “having set [them as] an example to those about to be impious” (2 Pet 2:6, LSV).

Ancient readers believed they could still see the evidence. The region around the southern end of the Dead Sea, desolate, salty, and sulfurous, was identified with the ruined cities. The Wisdom of Solomon says of them: “even to this day the waste land that smoketh is a testimony, and plants bearing fruit that never come to ripeness: and a standing pillar of salt is a monument of an unbelieving soul” (Wis 10:7, Brenton).41 Third Maccabees says that God made the Sodomites “an example to after generations” (3 Macc 2:5, Brenton).

  1. Abbott-Smith, s.v. δεῖγμα: “(cl.) a thing shown, a specimen. = cl. παράδ- (cf. ὑπόδ-, II Pe 2:6), an example (a warning): Ju 7.”↩︎

  2. Wisdom 10:7 and 3 Maccabees 2:5, in Brenton’s English Septuagint (eBible.org). The Greek of 3 Macc 2:5 reads παράδειγμα τοῖς ἐπιγινομένοις καταστήσας (“having established [them as] an example to those coming after”).↩︎

“undergoing the justice of continuous fire”

The present participle ὑπέχουσαι (hypechousai), “undergoing,” also unique in the New Testament, means “to undergo, suffer” a penalty.42 The noun δίκη (dikē), “justice,” here means the execution of a sentence, punishment. The phrase πυρὸς αἰωνίου (pyros aiōniou) is “eternal fire,” which the LSV renders “continuous fire,” in keeping with its consistent treatment of αἰώνιος (aiōnios).

Two senses may be combined. The visible, smoking desolation of the region was understood as an ongoing display of the fire that destroyed the cities. That fire also pointed beyond itself to the “continuous fire that has been prepared for the Devil and his messengers” (Matt 25:41, LSV). The cities are an example because their temporal destruction prefigures final judgment. Jesus made the same connection: “it will be more tolerable for the land of Sodom and Gomorrah in the day of judgment than for that city” (Matt 10:15, LSV).

  1. Abbott-Smith, s.v. ὑπέχω: “to undergo, suffer: δίκην, Ju 7 (Soph., Eur., al.).”↩︎

D. Original-Language Investigation

Greek Transliteration Basic meaning In context Notes
ὑπομιμνῄσκω hypomimnēskō remind “I intend to remind you” Calling to mind the known, not teaching the new.
εἰδότας eidotas knowing (perf. ptc. of οἶδα) “you once having known” Concessive: “although you know.”
ἅπαξ hapax once, once for all “once” Position varies in manuscripts (see deep dive).
Ἰησοῦς / κύριος Iēsous / kyrios Jesus / Lord “Jesus” Major variant (deep dive).
σῴζω sōzō save, deliver “having saved” Exodus salvation.
τὸ δεύτερον to deuteron the second time, afterward “secondly” Contrasts salvation and destruction.
ἀρχή archē beginning; rule, domain “principality” Assigned position and authority.
οἰκητήριον oikētērion dwelling, habitation “proper dwelling” Elsewhere only 2 Cor 5:2.
ἀΐδιος aidios everlasting “eternal bonds” Elsewhere only Rom 1:20. In later Greek not sharply distinct from αἰώνιος.43
ζόφος zophos gloom, deep darkness “under darkness” Homeric “gloom of the underworld”; also v. 13; 2 Pet 2:4, 17; Heb 12:18.
τηρέω tēreō keep, guard “did not keep … has kept” Wordplay.
ἐκπορνεύω ekporneuō give oneself to fornication “given themselves to whoredom” NT hapax; intensive.
σὰρξ ἑτέρα sarx hetera other/different flesh “other flesh” See deep dive on Sodom.
πρόκειμαι prokeimai be set forth, displayed “set before” Present tense: still displayed.
δεῖγμα deigma specimen, example “an example” NT hapax; 2 Pet 2:6 has ὑπόδειγμα.
δίκη dikē justice; penalty “the justice” Execution of a sentence.
ὑπέχω hypechō undergo, suffer “undergoing” NT hapax; with δίκην = “suffer punishment.”
αἰώνιος aiōnios eternal, age-long “continuous” LSV’s consistent rendering.

On “eternal” and “continuous.” The LSV’s “continuous” for αἰώνιος reflects a decision to translate the word by its root, αἰών (aiōn, “age”), rather than by the traditional “eternal.” Whether αἰώνιος in the New Testament means “unending” or “belonging to the age to come” has long been discussed, and the word can carry both ideas: the life and judgment of the coming age, which does not end. Readers should not build a doctrine of the duration of judgment on the LSV’s English choice alone, any more than on “eternal.” The theology must be built from the whole biblical witness.

Deep dive: “Jesus … saved a people out of Egypt” — the text of Jude 5

The Greek manuscripts of Jude 5 are a tangle. The two main points of variation are (a) the name of the one who saved the people and (b) the position of “once.”

The readings. According to the SBLGNT apparatus and its updated form citing NA28:44

Edition Reading of the key words
SBLGNT (2010), NA28 (2012) εἰδότας ὑμᾶς ἅπαξ πάντα ὅτι Ἰησοῦς λαὸν… (“knowing all things once, that Jesus…”)
NA27 (1993) …πάντα ὅτι ὁ κύριος ἅπαξ λαὸν… (“that the Lord, having once saved…”)
Westcott–Hort (1881), Tregelles …ἅπαξ πάντα ὅτι κύριος… (“that [the] Lord…”)
Robinson–Pierpont (Byzantine), TR εἰδότας ὑμᾶς ἅπαξ τοῦτο ὅτι ὁ κύριος… (“once knowing this, that the Lord…”)

The great majority of later Byzantine manuscripts read “the Lord.” According to the tallies in one modern edition, the Byzantine wording appears in about 69 percent of manuscripts and the wording with “Jesus” in a fraction of one percent.45 The earliest manuscripts, however, divide differently. “Jesus” is the reading of two of the most important early codices, Alexandrinus (A, fifth century) and Vaticanus (B, fourth century), together with other early witnesses. The earliest surviving papyrus of Jude, P72 (third or fourth century), has the remarkable reading θεὸς Χριστός, “God Christ.” Other manuscripts read “God.”46

How did the committee decide? When the United Bible Societies committee prepared the text that became NA27, the majority chose “the Lord.” Bruce Metzger’s Textual Commentary reports that two members of the committee, Metzger himself and Allen Wikgren, dissented, arguing that critical principles favored “Jesus” as the best-attested reading.47 By the time of NA28 (2012), which followed the Editio Critica Maior for the Catholic Letters, the editors had adopted “Jesus,” as the SBLGNT (2010) had already done independently.

The arguments.

For “Jesus”:

  1. Strong early attestation in important manuscripts and early translations.
  2. It is the harder reading. Scribes were more likely to change a surprising “Jesus” into an unproblematic “the Lord” than the reverse. A scribe copying “Jesus saved a people out of Egypt” might suppose that the historical Jesus was meant, find the statement impossible, and “correct” it.
  3. It explains the other readings. “Lord,” “God,” and “God Christ” can all be understood as attempts to resolve the difficulty of “Jesus.”
  4. A possible scribal mechanism. In manuscripts, sacred names were abbreviated: ΙΣ for “Jesus” and ΚΣ for “Lord.” These differ by one letter, so confusion in either direction was possible. This cuts both ways, though, and does not decide the question.

For “the Lord”:

  1. “Lord” is Jude’s usual word for God and Christ (vv. 4, 9, 14, 17, 21, 25). A reading that uses “Jesus” for the pre-existent Christ would be unusual.
  2. Some scholars judge that “Jesus” acting in the exodus is so unexpected that it is more likely a scribal slip (ΚΣ to ΙΣ) than an original reading.

Scholarly discussion continues. In 2007 Timo Flink argued that the reading “Jesus” has no parallel that a scribe could have imitated and is therefore hard to explain as an invention.48 Others have defended “the Lord.” What can be said with confidence is that “Jesus” is now the reading of the most widely used critical editions, of the SBLGNT on which this book is based, and of the LSV, and that it is very well attested.

What does it mean? If Jude wrote “Jesus,” he identifies the one who saved Israel from Egypt with Jesus Christ. He does not imply that the man from Nazareth lived in the second millennium BC. Rather, the one who became incarnate as Jesus already existed and acted as Israel’s deliverer. Christian theology calls this the pre-existence of Christ. It is affirmed elsewhere in the New Testament (John 1:1–3; 8:58; Phil 2:6–7; Col 1:15–17; Heb 1:2), and 1 Cor 10:4, 9 apply it specifically to the wilderness. Some interpreters connect it further with the mysterious “Messenger” of Exodus who leads Israel and of whom God says, “My Name [is] in His heart” (Exod 23:21, LSV). Jude does not develop the idea. He simply states it, as if it were already familiar to his readers.

If Jude wrote “the Lord,” he almost certainly still meant to evoke the same God whose lordship is now exercised by Jesus. Note that Jude calls Jesus “our only Master and Lord” in the previous verse (v. 4). On either reading, the warning is the same: the Lord who saves can also judge those who, having been saved, refuse to believe.

  1. Abbott-Smith, s.v. ἀΐδιος: “The etymological distinction between the meanings of the two words seems not to be retained in late Greek.”↩︎

  2. SBLGNT apparatus for Jude 5 (https://www.sblgnt.com/download/86-Jud-APP.txt) and the eBible.org SBLGNT edition’s updated notes: “Ἰησοῦς NA28 ] κύριος WH Treg; ὁ κύριος NIV RP; ὁ κύριος ἅπαξ NA27.”↩︎

  3. Text-Critical Greek NT notes (eBible.org grctcgnt): “υμας απαξ τουτο οτι ο κυριος 69.4% … υμας απαξ παντα οτι ιησους ɴᴀ ꜱʙʟ 0.2%.” These percentages count manuscripts without weighing them.↩︎

  4. The witness information for A, B, and P72 is drawn from the standard critical apparatuses and the discussions cited in nn. 11–12. It was not re-verified against a printed apparatus in this edition’s research (see the Bibliography’s verification notes).↩︎

  5. Bruce M. Metzger, A Textual Commentary on the Greek New Testament, 2nd ed. (Stuttgart: Deutsche Bibelgesellschaft, 1994), on Jude 5.↩︎

  6. Timo Flink, “Reconsidering the Text of Jude 5, 13, 15 and 18,” Filologia Neotestamentaria 20 (2007): 95–125. See also Philipp F. Bartholomä, “Did Jesus Save the People out of Egypt? A Re-examination of a Textual Problem in Jude 5,” Novum Testamentum 50 (2008): 143–58.↩︎

Deep dive: angels and the judgment traditions

Who were the angels “who did not keep their own principality, but left their proper dwelling”? The Old Testament offers only one story that fits: Genesis 6:1–4.

1 And it comes to pass that mankind has begun to multiply on the face of the ground, and daughters have been born to them, 2 and the sons of God see that they, the daughters of men, [are] beautiful, and they take women for themselves of all whom they have chosen. … 4 The giants were in the earth in those days, and even afterward, when sons of God come in to the daughters of men, and they have borne to them those who [were] the mighty from of old, the men of renown.

— Gen 6:1–4, LSV

Genesis does not say that the “sons of God” were punished, or even that they were angels. By the time of Jude, however, an elaborate Jewish tradition had grown up around these verses. Its fullest surviving form is the “Book of the Watchers,” 1 Enoch 1–36, usually dated to the third century BC or earlier. In it, two hundred angels called “Watchers” descend to earth, take human wives, father the giants, and teach humanity forbidden arts. God then sends his archangels to bind them:

And again the Lord said to Raphael: ‘Bind Azâzêl hand and foot, and cast him into the darkness: and make an opening in the desert … and cast him therein. And place upon him rough and jagged rocks, and cover him with darkness, and let him abide there for ever, and cover his face that he may not see light. And on the day of the great judgement he shall be cast into the fire.’

— 1 Enoch 10:4–6 (trans. R. H. Charles)49

And to Michael:

‘Go, bind Semjâzâ and his associates who have united themselves with women so as to have defiled themselves with them in all their uncleanness. And when their sons have slain one another … bind them fast for seventy generations in the valleys of the earth, till the day of their judgement and of their consummation …’

— 1 Enoch 10:11–12 (trans. R. H. Charles)

The correspondences with Jude 6 are close:

Jude 6 1 Enoch
“did not keep their own principality, but left their proper dwelling” The Watchers abandon heaven for earth (1 Enoch 6–7; 12:4; 15:3, 7)
“in eternal bonds” “Bind … hand and foot”; “bind them fast” (10:4, 12)
“under darkness” “cast him into the darkness … cover him with darkness” (10:4–5)
“until [the] judgment of [the] great day” “on the day of the great judgement” (10:6); “till the day of their judgement” (10:12)

Jude quotes 1 Enoch explicitly in vv. 14–15, so there is little doubt that he knew this tradition and expected his readers to know it. Second Peter’s parallel adds the term “Tartarus,” the Greek name for the deepest prison of the underworld: “God did not spare messengers having sinned, but having cast [them] down to Tartarus with chains of deepest gloom, delivered [them], having been reserved to judgment” (2 Pet 2:4, LSV). First Peter’s mysterious reference to “the spirits in prison, who sometime [ago] disobeyed … in [the] days of Noah” (1 Pet 3:19–20, LSV) probably belongs to the same circle of ideas.

The tradition also appears in other Jewish writings of the period. The Testament of Naphtali, discussed below, pairs the Watchers with Sodom exactly as Jude does. Sirach says that God “was not pacified toward the old giants” (Sir 16:7, Brenton). Third Maccabees recalls that God destroyed “the giants, who trusted in their strength and hardihood” (3 Macc 2:4, Brenton). The fall of the Watchers was a standard example of judgment in Second Temple Judaism, and Jude uses it the way his contemporaries did.

Other interpretations. Later Christian interpreters moved away from the angelic reading of Genesis 6. Augustine argued that the “sons of God” were “according to the flesh the sons of Seth” who intermarried with the ungodly line of Cain, and he insisted that angels, being spiritual, could not father children.50 Many readers have followed him, and others have read Jude 6 as a reference to an original fall of Satan and his angels before creation (cf. Rev 12:7–9), unconnected with Genesis 6. Both views are defensible as readings of Genesis in its own right. They fit Jude less well, because Jude’s vocabulary tracks 1 Enoch’s account of the Watchers, his next verse compares the angels’ sin to Sodom’s sexual sin, and he quotes 1 Enoch a few lines later. Clement of Alexandria, an early commentator, read Jude 6 of angels who “left their own habitation, meaning, that is, the heaven and the stars,” and took the “chains” as “the loss of the honour in which they had stood.”51

What Jude affirms, and what he does not. Jude affirms that some heavenly beings rebelled, that they are under restraint, and that they await final judgment. He does not provide a full angelology, a description of their prison, or a timetable. He uses a story his readers knew to make a single point: even angels who abandoned their God-given place did not escape judgment. How much of the surrounding Enochic narrative Jude believed to be historical is a separate question, taken up in Chapter 8.

  1. R. H. Charles, The Book of Enoch (London: SPCK, 1917), 1 Enoch 10:4–6, 11–12; Wikisource transcription. Charles’s typographical marks are omitted.↩︎

  2. Augustine, City of God 15.23 (NPNF¹ 2; https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/120115.htm).↩︎

  3. Clement of Alexandria, Adumbrationes on Jude 6, ANF 2.↩︎

Deep dive: Sodom and Gomorrah

Sodom and Gomorrah became the Bible’s standard example of total destruction. Moses warns Israel that disobedience would leave the land “like the overthrow of Sodom and Gomorrah, Admah and Zeboim” (Deut 29:23, LSV). Isaiah calls Jerusalem’s rulers “you rulers of Sodom” (Isa 1:10, LSV). Jesus says the coming of the Son of Man will be like the days of Lot, when “He rained fire and brimstone from the sky, and destroyed all” (Luke 17:29, LSV). Paul and Peter both cite the cities. But what, according to the Bible and early Jewish tradition, was Sodom’s sin?

The biblical evidence.

  • Genesis 18–19. The outcry against Sodom was already great before the angels arrived: “The cry of Sodom and Gomorrah—because great; and their sin—because exceedingly grievous” (Gen 18:20, LSV). The episode in Gen 19:4–5 shows the city’s character in action. The men of the city, “from young even to aged,” surround Lot’s house and demand that the visitors be brought out “and we know them” (LSV), that is, for sexual assault. The scene combines violent sexual aggression, the violation of guests whom hospitality required Lot to protect, and the fact that the guests were angels.
  • Ezekiel 16:49–50. Ezekiel names the sins of “your sister Sodom” as “Arrogancy, fullness of bread, and quiet ease,” the failure to strengthen “the hand of the afflicted and needy,” and adds, “they are haughty and do abomination before Me” (LSV). Ezekiel’s emphasis is on pride, complacent wealth, and neglect of the poor, with “abomination” as a further charge.
  • Isaiah 1:10 and Jeremiah 23:14 use Sodom as a byword for injustice and moral corruption in Jerusalem.

The Second Temple evidence. Jewish writers of Jude’s era gave the story different emphases:

  • Pride and insolence: God “abhorred them for their pride” (Sir 16:8, Brenton). God destroyed “the Sodomites, those workers of exceeding iniquity” for their pride and insolence (3 Macc 2:5).
  • Hostility to strangers and sexual practices: Josephus writes that “the Sodomites grew proud, on account of their riches and great wealth; they became unjust towards men, and impious towards God … they hated strangers, and abused themselves with Sodomitical practices.”52
  • Gluttony, debauchery, and same-sex relations: Philo of Alexandria describes Sodom as “full of innumerable iniquities, and especially of gluttony and debauchery,” and says that its men “not only … went mad after women, and defile[d] the marriage bed of others, but also those who were men lusted after one another.”53
  • Changing the order of nature, paired with the Watchers: The Testament of Naphtali urges its readers to recognize the Lord in creation, “that you become not as Sodom, which changed the order of its nature, in like manner also the Watchers changed the order of their nature, whom also the Lord cursed at the flood.”54

The last text is especially important for Jude, because it makes exactly Jude’s pairing. Sodom and the Watchers are linked as two instances of transgressing the order of nature God established. The Watchers crossed from heaven to earth to unite with human women. Sodom’s men sought to violate heavenly visitors. Jude’s “in like manner to these … having gone after other flesh” fits this pattern precisely.

What “other flesh” means in Jude. Three readings are found among interpreters:

  1. Angelic flesh. “Other” (ἑτέρα, hetera) means “of a different kind.” The men of Sodom pursued sexual union with beings who were not human, the counterpart of the angels’ pursuit of human women. This is the reading most modern critical commentators favor, because it makes the best sense of “in like manner to these” and of the Testament of Naphtali parallel.
  2. Same-sex relations. “Other” means “contrary to nature,” and Jude refers to homosexual acts, which Philo and Josephus associated with Sodom. This reading is traditional in much Christian interpretation.
  3. Sexual immorality in general, with the angelic and same-sex dimensions both included in the description of the city’s excess.

The first reading best explains Jude’s comparison, and this book adopts it as the primary sense. Two cautions follow from it. First, Jude 7 should not be made to carry more than it says. The phrase’s main reference is to the attempted violation of angels, and Jude does not address modern questions about committed same-sex relationships. Those questions are usually argued from other texts (for example Lev 18:22 and Rom 1:26–27), and readers should weigh those passages on their own terms. Second, the opposite overcorrection is equally mistaken: Jude clearly condemns sexual immorality (“having given themselves to whoredom”). Sodom in Jude is not merely a symbol of inhospitality, even though inhospitality and violence were part of its sin. Honest reading lets Jude say what he says and no more.

Why Sodom fits Jude’s argument. Jude’s three examples move from God’s people (Israel), to heavenly beings (angels), to pagan cities (Sodom). The circle widens to show that judgment reaches every order of creation. All three share one feature: they left the place God gave them. Israel left trust for unbelief, the angels left heaven for earth, and Sodom left the order of creation for the pursuit of “other flesh.” The intruders, Jude will say, do “in like manner” (v. 8).

  1. Josephus, Antiquities 1.11.1 (Whiston), Project Gutenberg #2848.↩︎

  2. Philo, On Abraham 133–135, trans. C. D. Yonge, https://www.earlyjewishwritings.com/text/philo/book22.html.↩︎

  3. Testament of Naphtali 3:4–5, trans. R. Sinker, ANF 8 (https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0801.htm).↩︎

E. The Historical and Cultural World

The exodus in Jewish memory. The exodus from Egypt was the central saving event of Israel’s story, recited at Passover, celebrated in the Psalms, and used by the prophets as the pattern for future deliverance. The wilderness generation’s failure was equally remembered, as a warning. Psalm 95, sung in synagogue and church, ends with God’s oath: “Forty years I am weary of the generation, … Where I swore in My anger” that they would not enter his rest (Ps 95:10–11, LSV). Jude’s readers would have known both sides of the story.

Angels in Second Temple Judaism. Jewish thought between the Testaments developed a rich angelology: named archangels (Michael, Gabriel, Raphael, Uriel), angelic hierarchies, guardian angels of nations, and fallen angels. The Dead Sea Scrolls, 1 Enoch, Jubilees, and other writings show how widely these ideas were held. Jude’s casual reference to rebellious angels and, in v. 9, to Michael the archangel shows that he and his readers shared this world of thought.

The Dead Sea region. The southern end of the Dead Sea, with its salt formations, bitumen, and barren landscape, was identified in antiquity with the destroyed cities of the plain. Wisdom 10:7, quoted above, speaks of its “waste land that smoketh” and its pillar of salt. Josephus also describes the region.55 For Jude’s readers, the “example” was not only a story but a place.

  1. Josephus, Antiquities 1.11.4 and Jewish War 4.8.4, describe the region of the Dead Sea and Lot’s wife (cited from prior knowledge; not re-verified in this edition).↩︎

F. Connections Across Scripture

1 Corinthians 10:1–11 (close thematic parallel). Paul’s use of the wilderness generation is the closest parallel to Jude 5. All Israel “passed through the sea … and all ate the same spiritual food,” yet “in the most of them God was not well pleased, for they were strewn in the wilderness” (1 Cor 10:1, 3, 5, LSV). Paul identifies the rock as “the Christ” (10:4) and warns against tempting “the Christ” (10:9). His conclusion, “these things happened to those persons as types, and they were written for our admonition” (10:11, LSV), is exactly Jude’s use of the story. The texts share a tradition of interpretation, but neither is likely quoting the other.

Hebrews 3:16–19 (close thematic parallel). “Was it not with those who sinned, whose carcasses fell in the wilderness? … they were not able to enter in because of unbelief” (Heb 3:17, 19, LSV). Hebrews stresses unbelief just as Jude does (“those not having believed”).

2 Peter 2:4–9 (literary parallel). Peter’s version lists angels, the flood, and Sodom, in biblical order. It adds Noah and Lot as examples of the righteous rescued and concludes: “the LORD has known to rescue [the] pious out of temptation, and to keep [the] unrighteous being punished to [the] day of judgment” (2 Pet 2:9, LSV). The relationship to Jude is discussed in the Introduction and Appendix C.

Matthew 25:41; Revelation 20:10 (shared theme). The “continuous fire that has been prepared for the Devil and his messengers” (Matt 25:41, LSV) connects the fate of rebellious angels and the fire of judgment, the same two ideas that Jude 6–7 places side by side.

Exodus 23:20–21 (possible background). The Messenger in whom God’s Name dwells and who leads Israel through the wilderness has been connected by some interpreters with Jude’s “Jesus” in v. 5. This is a suggestive connection, not a demonstrated allusion.

G. Scholarly and Theological Questions

1. “Jesus” or “the Lord” in v. 5? Discussed in the deep dive. The reading “Jesus” is strongly attested and is now printed in the main critical editions, but a minority of scholars still prefers “the Lord.”

2. Can the redeemed be destroyed? Jude’s first example concerns people who were genuinely saved from Egypt and later destroyed for unbelief. Christian traditions read this differently. Some see in it a warning that those who were once part of the redeemed community can fall away. Others see it as a type: physical deliverance from Egypt was real, but not every member of the delivered people had saving faith, and so the warning targets those whose membership in the church is not matched by faith. Jude’s purpose is practical rather than systematic. He warns the readers not to trust in privilege, and he assures them that God is able to keep them (v. 24).

3. Does Jude endorse the Watcher story as history? Jude certainly uses the tradition, and his argument assumes its point. Whether he regarded every detail of 1 Enoch’s narrative as historical cannot be determined from his brief allusion. The question is discussed further in Chapter 8.

4. What was Sodom’s sin? Discussed in the deep dive. Jude’s own emphasis is on sexual immorality and the pursuit of “other flesh,” understood in light of the angel parallel. Other biblical texts emphasize pride, wealth, injustice, and inhospitality. A full biblical portrait includes all of these.

5. “Eternal” or “continuous” fire? The question of what αἰώνιος means, and of the nature and duration of final judgment, is a larger theological discussion that Jude’s verse alone cannot settle. Jude’s own point is that Sodom’s destruction is a visible, lasting example of judgment that should give the reader pause.

H. The Passage Within Jude

Verses 5–7 are the first of Jude’s “texts,” the evidence that the intruders’ condemnation was “written beforehand” (v. 4). The next verse begins “In like manner” (ὁμοίως, homoiōs) and applies the examples: “those dreaming indeed defile the flesh, and they disregard lordship, and they speak evil of glorious [ones]” (v. 8). The three sins of v. 8 correspond loosely to the three examples. Defiling the flesh corresponds to Sodom. Disregarding lordship corresponds to the angels who left their place and to Israel’s rebellion. Slandering glorious ones prepares for the contrast with Michael in v. 9.

The passage also develops the letter’s key word: “keep.” The angels “did not keep” and are “kept” (v. 6). The readers, “kept” by God (v. 1), will be urged to “keep” themselves (v. 21). The examples of vv. 5–7 are not only threats against the intruders. They are also mirrors for the readers, showing what it would mean to leave the place God has given.

I. Contemporary Understanding

These reflections are suggested applications, distinct from the original meaning.

Privilege is not protection. Jude’s first example is directed at people who belong to the community of the saved. Church membership, a dramatic conversion story, or a heritage of faith do not by themselves guarantee faithfulness. Jude’s warning may be most needed in communities that feel most secure.

The goodness of boundaries. All three examples involve leaving a God-given place. Modern culture often treats limits as obstacles to self-realization. Jude’s examples suggest that boundaries such as the place of trust, of creaturely station, and of the created order can be gifts that protect and do not merely restrict. This idea can be misused to justify unjust social hierarchies, which Jude does not address. Applied to the believer’s relationship with God, though, it invites reflection on the freedom found in remaining where God has placed us: in his love (v. 21).

Reading hard texts honestly. Jude 7 is often quoted in modern debates. Responsible readers will resist both the temptation to make it say more than it does and the temptation to make it say less. That discipline is itself a form of “contending for the faith.”

Christ in the whole story. If Jude 5 identifies Jesus as Israel’s deliverer, Christians may read the Old Testament as the story of the same Lord they know in the Gospels. That conviction has shaped Christian worship and interpretation from the beginning.

J. Reflection and Discussion

  1. Why do you think Jude began his examples with God’s own people rather than with pagan Sodom? What effect might that order have had on his readers?
  2. What difference does it make to your understanding of Jesus if Jude 5 says that Jesus saved Israel from Egypt?
  3. The angels “did not keep their own principality” and are now “kept” for judgment. How does this wordplay illuminate the relationship between faithfulness and security?
  4. Jude, Ezekiel, Josephus, and Philo describe Sodom’s sin differently. Why might different writers emphasize different aspects of the same story? What are the risks of focusing on only one?
  5. Jude says his readers already know these things but need reminding. What truths do you know well but most need to be reminded of?
