  • Testament of Naphtali 3:2, ANF 8.
  • Clement of Alexandria, Adumbrationes on Jude 13, ANF 2.
  • LXX Isa 57:20 (Brenton’s Greek Septuagint).
  • Abbott-Smith, s.vv. ἐπαφρίζω, πλανήτης, ζόφος.
  • Bauckham, Jude, 2 Peter, on vv. 12–13 (cited from prior knowledge).

Chapter 8. Enoch, the Seventh from Adam (Jude 14–15)

A. The Text

14 And Enoch, the seventh from Adam, also prophesied to these, saying, “Behold, the LORD has come with myriads of His holy ones, 15 to do judgment against all, and to convict all their impious ones, concerning all their works of impiety that they did impiously, and concerning all the harsh [words] that impious sinners spoke against Him.”

— Jude 14–15, LSV

B. Plain-English Orientation

Jude now quotes a prophecy attributed to Enoch, the ancient patriarch who “habitually walks with God, and he is not, for God has taken him” (Gen 5:24, LSV). The prophecy announces that the Lord comes with countless holy angels to judge everyone and to convict the ungodly of all their ungodly deeds and of all the harsh words they have spoken against him. Jude says that Enoch prophesied “to,” or about, “these,” the intruders. He sees the ancient prophecy as describing people in his own community.

The quotation comes from the opening chapter of the book known today as 1 Enoch, a Jewish writing composed in stages over the last centuries before Christ. This is the only explicit quotation in Jude, and the clearest case in the New Testament of a Jewish writing from outside the Hebrew Bible being quoted and introduced as prophecy. It has raised questions from the second century to the present: Did Jude regard 1 Enoch as Scripture? What does his quotation imply about the book’s authority? This chapter looks first at what the quotation says and how Jude uses it, and then turns to the questions of canon.

C. Phrase-by-Phrase Exploration

“And Enoch, the seventh from Adam”

Genesis 5 lists the generations from Adam to Noah: Adam, Seth, Enosh, Kenan, Mahalalel, Jared, Enoch (cf. 1 Chr 1:1–3). Counting Adam as the first, as ancient counting usually did, Enoch is the seventh: ἕβδομος ἀπὸ Ἀδάμ (hebdomos apo Adam), “seventh from Adam.” The phrase appears in 1 Enoch itself, where Noah refers to his great-grandfather as “taken up, the seventh from Adam, the first man whom the Lord of Spirits created” (1 Enoch 60:8), and where Enoch says, “I was born the seventh in the first week” (1 Enoch 93:3).115

The description does three things:

  1. It identifies him. There was another Enoch, Cain’s son, after whom Cain named a city (Gen 4:17). Jude means the righteous Enoch of Seth’s line.
  2. It stresses antiquity. Enoch lived before the flood, in the seventh generation of humanity. His prophecy is among the oldest words of God imaginable. Calvin saw this as Jude’s purpose: “he says expressly that he was the seventh from Adam, in order to commend the antiquity of the prophecy, because it existed in the world before the flood.”116
  3. It may suggest completeness. Seven was a number of fullness and perfection in Jewish thought. The “seventh” from Adam was a person of special standing, the one who “walks with God” and was taken by God. Jude does not draw attention to the symbolism, but his readers may have felt it.

Genesis says that Enoch “habitually walks with God” and that “God has taken him” (Gen 5:24, LSV). Hebrews adds: “By faith Enoch was translated—not to see death … for before his translation he had been testified to—that he had pleased God well” (Heb 11:5, LSV). A man so close to God and taken from the earth without dying was an ideal recipient of revelation, and Jewish tradition attributed to him visions of heaven, of the secrets of the cosmos, and of the final judgment.

  1. 1 Enoch 60:8; 93:3, trans. R. H. Charles (1917), Wikisource.↩︎

  2. John Calvin, Commentaries on the Catholic Epistles, trans. John Owen (1855), on Jude 14.↩︎

“also prophesied to these”

The verb προεφήτευσεν (proephēteusen), “prophesied,” is significant. Jude does not say that Enoch “wrote” or that “it is written.” He says that Enoch prophesied. The word “also” (καί, kai) links this prophecy to the other evidence Jude has presented: Israel’s history, the angels, Sodom, Michael, Cain, Balaam, and Korah all testify against the intruders, and so does Enoch.

The dative τούτοις (toutois), “to these,” is best understood as “about these” or “with reference to these.” Jude does not claim that Enoch foresaw these particular individuals by name. He claims that the people Enoch described, the “impious” whom the Lord will judge, are people like these. This is the same way of reading prophecy found in the Dead Sea Scrolls and elsewhere in the New Testament: ancient words are understood as speaking to the reader’s own time.

“saying, ‘Behold, the LORD has come’”

The quotation opens with Ἰδού (Idou), “Behold!” or “Look!”, a prophetic call to attention.

“The LORD” translates κύριος (kyrios). The LSV prints it in capitals because the reference is to the God of Israel. In 1 Enoch, the subject is God: “And behold! He cometh with ten thousands of His holy ones” (1 Enoch 1:9, trans. Charles). In Jude, however, “Lord” most often refers to Jesus (vv. 4, 17, 21; also, on some readings, v. 5). Many interpreters think Jude understands the coming Lord of Enoch’s prophecy to be Jesus Christ at his return. That would be a further instance of the pattern seen in v. 5 and v. 4: language about the God of Israel applied to Jesus. The New Testament repeatedly pictures Jesus coming “with all His holy ones” (1 Thess 3:13, LSV) and “with messengers of His power” (2 Thess 1:7, LSV).

The verb ἦλθεν (ēlthen) is in the aorist, “came” (LSV: “has come”). In the context of a prophecy of future judgment, the past tense expresses certainty. Hebrew prophets often spoke of future events in the perfect tense, as though already accomplished, and Jude’s Greek reproduces the effect. Some interpreters suggest that the past tense reflects the Aramaic or Greek form of Enoch that Jude knew.

“with myriads of His holy ones”

The phrase ἐν ἁγίαις μυριάσιν αὐτοῦ (en hagiais myriasin autou), “with his holy myriads,” means “with tens of thousands of his holy ones.” A μυριάς (myrias) is ten thousand, and the plural means “countless thousands.” The “holy ones” are angels, the heavenly army that accompanies God in his appearing.

The image goes back to the theophany at Sinai, as described in Moses’ blessing: “YHWH has come from Sinai, / And has risen from Seir for them; He has shone from Mount Paran, / And has come [with] myriads of holy ones” (Deut 33:2, LSV). 1 Enoch’s opening deliberately echoes this. It describes God coming to “tread upon the earth, (even) on Mount Sinai” (1 Enoch 1:4).117 Zechariah also foresees the day when “my God YHWH has come in, / All holy ones [are] with You” (Zech 14:5, LSV). Jesus speaks of the Son of Man coming “in His glory, and all the holy messengers with Him” (Matt 25:31, LSV).

  1. 1 Enoch 1:3–4, trans. R. H. Charles (1917): “The Holy Great One will come forth from His dwelling, And the eternal God will tread upon the earth, (even) on Mount Sinai.”↩︎

“to do judgment against all”

The purpose of the coming is ποιῆσαι κρίσιν κατὰ πάντων (poiēsai krisin kata pantōn), “to execute judgment against all.” The word “all” (πᾶς, pas) is repeated four times in the quotation: judgment on all, convicting all, concerning all their works, concerning all the harsh words. The judgment is universal and complete. Nothing escapes.

“and to convict all their impious ones”

The verb ἐλέγξαι (elenxai) means “to expose, convict, prove guilty.” It is the language of a courtroom in which guilt is demonstrated. Jesus says that the Spirit “will convict the world concerning sin, and concerning righteousness, and concerning judgment” (John 16:8, LSV).

A textual note. The object of “convict” varies in the manuscripts:

Edition Reading Translation
SBLGNT, WH, Tregelles πάντας τοὺς ἀσεβεῖς “all the impious”
Robinson–Pierpont (Byzantine), TR πάντας τοὺς ἀσεβεῖς αὐτῶν “all their impious ones” (LSV)
NA28 πᾶσαν ψυχήν “every soul”

The NA28 reading, “every soul,” corresponds more closely to the wording of 1 Enoch 1:9, “to convict all flesh” in Charles’s translation, and the NA28 editors judged it original. The SBLGNT editors preferred “all the impious,” which fits Jude’s emphasis on the word “impious.” The LSV follows the Byzantine reading with “their.” The difference does not affect the sense of the passage: on any reading, the ungodly are convicted.118 (The TR also reads ἐξελέγξαι, a strengthened form of the verb.)

  1. SBLGNT apparatus and eBible.org SBLGNT notes: “πάντας τοὺς ἀσεβεῖς WH Treg NIV ] + αὐτῶν RP; πᾶσαν ψυχὴν NA28.” The Text-Critical Greek NT notes give the Byzantine reading in about 80% of manuscripts and πᾶσαν ψυχήν in under 1%; such counts do not determine originality.↩︎

“concerning all their works of impiety that they did impiously”

Here the quotation reaches its most emphatic point. The Greek περὶ πάντων τῶν ἔργων ἀσεβείας αὐτῶν ὧν ἠσέβησαν (peri pantōn tōn ergōn asebeias autōn hōn ēsebēsan) uses the root ἀσεβ- (aseb-), “ungodly,” twice in quick succession: “their ungodly works which they ungodly did.” With “impious” earlier and “impious sinners” at the end, the word family appears four times in a single sentence. The verb ἠσέβησαν (ēsebēsan), “they acted impiously,” occurs nowhere else in the New Testament.

The repetition is deliberate. Jude introduced the intruders as ἀσεβεῖς, “impious,” in v. 4. Now, in the words of the ancient prophecy, the same word rings out four times. Jude’s earlier description of them was not merely his own opinion. It was the language of prophecy.

“and concerning all the harsh [words] that impious sinners spoke against Him”

The last clause shifts from deeds to words: περὶ πάντων τῶν σκληρῶν ὧν ἐλάλησαν κατ’ αὐτοῦ ἁμαρτωλοὶ ἀσεβεῖς (peri pantōn tōn sklērōn hōn elalēsan kat’ autou hamartōloi asebeis), “concerning all the hard things that ungodly sinners spoke against him.” The adjective σκληρός (sklēros) means “hard, harsh, rough.” In context it means defiant, insolent speech against God.

1 Enoch returns to this theme repeatedly. Immediately after the prophecy, it accuses sinners: “ye have turned away and spoken proud and hard words / With your impure mouths against His greatness” (1 Enoch 5:4). It speaks of a valley of judgment for those “who utter with their lips against the Lord unseemly words and of His glory speak hard things” (27:2), and it charges, “ye spake proud and insolent words against His righteousness” (101:3).119

The emphasis on speech connects the quotation with Jude’s charges. The intruders “speak evil of glorious [ones]” (v. 8) and of “whatever [things] they have not known” (v. 10). In the next verse Jude will add that “their mouth speaks swollen words” (v. 16), and the apostles foretold “scoffers” (v. 18). Sins of speech run through the letter.

  1. 1 Enoch 5:4; 27:2; 101:3, trans. R. H. Charles (1917), Wikisource.↩︎

Comparing Jude 14–15 with 1 Enoch 1:9

Placing Jude’s quotation beside R. H. Charles’s translation of 1 Enoch 1:9 (which Charles made from the Ethiopic, compared with the Greek) shows both close agreement and some differences:

Jude 14–15 (LSV) 1 Enoch 1:9 (trans. Charles)
“Behold, the LORD has come” “And behold! He cometh”
“with myriads of His holy ones,” “with ten thousands of ⌈His⌉ holy ones”
“to do judgment against all,” “To execute judgement upon all,”
— “And to destroy ⌈all⌉ the ungodly:”
“and to convict all their impious ones,” “And to convict all flesh”
“concerning all their works of impiety that they did impiously,” “Of all the works ⌈of their ungodliness⌉ which they have ungodly committed,”
“and concerning all the harsh [words] that impious sinners spoke against Him.” “⌈And of all the hard things which⌉ ungodly sinners ⌈have spoken⌉ against Him.”

The differences are of three kinds:

  1. Jude names “the Lord” where 1 Enoch has “He.” This may be Jude’s clarification, and it opens the way to applying the prophecy to Christ.
  2. Jude lacks the line “And to destroy all the ungodly,” found in the Ethiopic and Greek of 1 Enoch.
  3. “All flesh” vs. “the impious.” The NA28 reading, “every soul,” is close to 1 Enoch’s “all flesh,” which is one reason the NA28 editors adopted it.

The Aramaic original of 1 Enoch 1:9 is partly preserved in a fragment from Qumran Cave 4, known as 4Q204. J. T. Milik, who published the Aramaic Enoch fragments, judged that Jude’s wording stands closer to the Aramaic than either the Greek or the Ethiopic versions do.120 Richard Bauckham has argued that Jude knew the Greek version but made his own rendering with an eye on the Aramaic.121 These detailed reconstructions should be treated as scholarly proposals. What is clear is that Jude quotes a form of the text very close to the one we know, adapting it to his purpose.

  1. J. T. Milik, The Books of Enoch: Aramaic Fragments of Qumrân Cave 4 (Oxford: Clarendon, 1976). Milik’s judgment is reported here from secondary discussions and was not checked against his volume for this edition.↩︎

  2. Bauckham, Jude, 2 Peter, on vv. 14–15 (cited from prior knowledge).↩︎

Deep dive: The Book of Enoch and the biblical canon

Jude’s quotation of Enoch raises one of the most interesting questions in the history of the Bible: what does it mean when a biblical writer quotes a book that is not itself in most Bibles?

1. What is 1 Enoch?

“1 Enoch” (also called “Ethiopic Enoch”) is a collection of five main sections, written by Jewish authors at different times between roughly the third century BC and the turn of the era:

Section Chapters Content Approximate date
Book of the Watchers 1–36 Coming judgment; fall of the Watchers; Enoch’s journeys 3rd century BC or earlier
Book of Parables (Similitudes) 37–71 Visions of the “Son of Man” / “Elect One” and judgment 1st century BC–1st century AD (debated)
Astronomical Book 72–82 Movements of sun, moon, and stars; calendar 3rd century BC or earlier
Book of Dreams 83–90 Visions of the flood and of Israel’s history (the “Animal Apocalypse”) 2nd century BC
Epistle of Enoch 91–108 Exhortations, woes, the “Apocalypse of Weeks” 2nd century BC

The quotation in Jude comes from the opening of the Book of the Watchers, one of the oldest parts.122

How the text survives. Aramaic fragments of most parts of 1 Enoch were found among the Dead Sea Scrolls in Qumran Cave 4 (manuscripts known as 4Q201–4Q212). The oldest are dated by their handwriting to the early second century BC. The Book of Parables is not represented at Qumran, which is one reason it is often dated later. A Greek manuscript of chapters 1–32, found at Akhmim (ancient Panopolis) in Egypt and published in 1892, preserves the Greek translation, and other Greek fragments survive. The complete book survives only in Ge’ez, the classical language of Ethiopia, in which it has been transmitted as part of the Bible.123

Canonical status today. 1 Enoch is part of the Old Testament in the Ethiopian Orthodox Tewahedo Church and the Eritrean Orthodox Tewahedo Church. It is used by the Beta Israel (Ethiopian Jewish) community. It is not part of the canon of Rabbinic Judaism or of other Christian churches, whether Catholic, Eastern Orthodox, Oriental Orthodox outside Ethiopia and Eritrea, or Protestant.124

  1. The division and approximate dating follow the standard scholarly consensus, as summarized in Nickelsburg, 1 Enoch 1, and “Book of Enoch,” Wikipedia. Dates for individual sections, especially the Book of Parables, remain debated.↩︎

  2. “Book of Enoch,” Wikipedia (accessed 7 November 2026): Aramaic fragments 4Q201–4Q212; Akhmim Greek codex of chapters 1–32 published in 1892; complete text only in Ge’ez.↩︎

  3. Ibid.↩︎

2. How did early Christians respond to Jude’s quotation?

The ancient church took up the question directly, and its answers diverged:

  • Tertullian (c. 200) argued that the Book of Enoch should be accepted. He acknowledged that “the Scripture of Enoch … is not received by some, because it is not admitted into the Jewish canon either,” but he insisted that “Enoch possesses a testimony in the Apostle Jude.”125 For Tertullian, Jude’s quotation proved Enoch’s authority.
  • Jerome (late fourth century) reported the opposite effect: “because in it he quotes from the apocryphal Book of Enoch it is rejected by many. Nevertheless by age and use it has gained authority and is reckoned among the Holy Scriptures.”126 For some, Jude’s quotation of Enoch counted against Jude.
  • Augustine (early fifth century) took a middle position: “We cannot deny that Enoch, the seventh from Adam, left some divine writings, for this is asserted by the Apostle Jude in his canonical epistle. But it is not without reason that these writings have no place in that canon of Scripture which was preserved in the temple of the Hebrew people.” He explained that such books are excluded because of their “obscure origin” and because “though there is some truth in these apocryphal writings, yet they contain so many false statements.”127
  • John Calvin (sixteenth century) suggested that Jude might not have taken the prophecy from a book at all: “I rather think that this prophecy was unwritten, than that it was taken from an apocryphal book; for it may have been delivered down by memory to posterity by the ancients.”128

  1. Tertullian, De cultu feminarum 1.3, ANF 4 (https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/0402.htm).↩︎

  2. Jerome, De viris illustribus 4, NPNF² 3 (https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/2708.htm).↩︎

  3. Augustine, City of God 15.23, NPNF¹ 2 (https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/120115.htm).↩︎

  4. Calvin, Commentaries on the Catholic Epistles, on Jude 14.↩︎

3. How can Jude’s use be understood?

Modern interpreters generally take one of the following views. They are presented here with their strengths and weaknesses.

(a) Jude regarded 1 Enoch as Scripture. On this view, Jude, like some Jews of his time (the Qumran community preserved many copies of Enoch), treated 1 Enoch as authoritative revelation.

  • Strength: Jude calls the passage prophecy and uses it as decisive evidence.
  • Weakness: Jude does not use the formulas by which New Testament writers usually introduce Scripture (“it is written,” “the Scripture says”). And the book was not, as far as we know, part of the scriptures generally recognized in the synagogues.

(b) Jude regarded this prophecy as a genuine word of Enoch, without endorsing the whole book. On this view, Jude believed that the historical Enoch spoke these words, which had been preserved in tradition and recorded in the Enochic literature, without implying that everything in 1 Enoch was inspired.

  • Strength: This view takes Jude’s “Enoch … prophesied” seriously and matches Augustine’s position.
  • Weakness: Modern historical study regards 1 Enoch as written many centuries after any historical Enoch. Those who hold this view must either dispute that conclusion or argue that the book preserves an ancient tradition. Both are possible but hard to demonstrate.

(c) Jude quoted a respected text that his readers knew, as true in what it says, without making a claim about its authorship or canonical status. On this view, “prophesied” means that the passage speaks truly about the future judgment, as indeed the Old Testament does in many places (Deut 33:2; Zech 14:5; Dan 7:10). Jude quotes it because it was familiar and authoritative to his readers, and possibly to the intruders themselves, if they appealed to visionary literature.

  • Strength: The New Testament elsewhere quotes non-biblical sources for true statements without implying their inspiration. Paul quotes a Greek poet at Athens: “as certain of your poets have also said: For we are also His offspring” (Acts 17:28, LSV). Writing to Titus, he even calls the Cretan poet Epimenides “a prophet of their own” before quoting him and adding, “This testimony is true” (Titus 1:12–13, LSV). He quotes a proverb found in the Greek playwright Menander: “evil communications corrupt good manners” (1 Cor 15:33, LSV). Second Timothy names Moses’ opponents “Jannes and Jambres” (2 Tim 3:8, LSV), names known from Jewish tradition and not from the Old Testament.
  • Weakness: Jude’s language is stronger than these parallels. He says Enoch “prophesied,” and he treats the prophecy as applying directly to the intruders.

4. Principles worth holding

Whatever view one takes, several principles help readers think clearly:

  1. Quotation is not canonization. That a biblical author quotes a book does not make that book Scripture. Paul’s quotations of pagan poets do not make those poets canonical. The question of Jude’s view of Enoch must be decided on its own evidence.
  2. The quoted statement can be true without endorsing everything in the source. Jude affirms that the Lord will come with his holy ones in judgment, a truth taught throughout Scripture. Nothing in his quotation requires readers to accept the astronomical calendar of 1 Enoch 72–82 or every detail of its angelology.
  3. Jude’s own authority does not depend on the answer. The church accepted Jude into its canon in full knowledge that Jude quoted Enoch, as Tertullian, Jerome, and Augustine show. Christians who accept Jude as Scripture can hold different views of 1 Enoch.
  4. The study of 1 Enoch illuminates the New Testament. Whatever its canonical status, 1 Enoch helps modern readers understand ideas that were current in Jude’s world: the Watchers, the judgment of fallen angels, the “Son of Man,” and the coming of God with holy myriads. Reading it is no threat to faith. It is a way of hearing the New Testament in its own historical accent.
  5. Respect for other traditions. For Ethiopian and Eritrean Orthodox Christians, 1 Enoch is Scripture. Other Christians can respect that tradition while explaining their own canon, and can recognize that the boundaries of the canon were not settled in the same way or at the same time everywhere.

D. Original-Language Investigation

Greek Transliteration Basic meaning In context Notes
ἕβδομος hebdomos seventh “the seventh from Adam” Inclusive counting; 1 Enoch 60:8; 93:3.
προφητεύω prophēteuō prophesy “prophesied” Not “wrote” or “it is written.”
τούτοις toutois to/about these “to these” Dative of reference.
ἰδού idou behold, look “Behold” Prophetic call to attention.
ἦλθεν ēlthen came “has come” Aorist expressing certainty.
μυριάς myrias ten thousand “myriads” Countless angelic host.
ἅγιος hagios holy “holy ones” Angels (Deut 33:2; Zech 14:5).
κρίσις krisis judgment “judgment”
ἐλέγχω elenchō expose, convict “to convict” TR: ἐξελέγχω.
ἀσεβής / ἀσέβεια / ἀσεβέω asebēs / asebeia / asebeō impious / impiety / act impiously “impious … impiety … did impiously” Fourfold repetition; ἀσεβέω is an NT hapax.
σκληρός sklēros hard, harsh “harsh [words]” Insolent speech; cf. 1 Enoch 5:4; 27:2.
ἁμαρτωλός hamartōlos sinner “sinners”
πᾶσαν ψυχήν pasan psychēn every soul (NA28 reading) Alternative to “all the impious.”

On “prophesied.” The verb προφητεύω has a range of uses in the New Testament: foretelling (Matt 15:7), speaking God’s message (1 Cor 14:1–5), and even mocking demands to “prophesy” (Mark 14:65). In Jude, it clearly means that Enoch’s words convey a true message about the future judgment of the ungodly. Whether it also implies a judgment about the book’s authorship and authority is the question discussed in the deep dive.

E. The Historical and Cultural World

Enoch traditions in Judaism. Enoch fascinated Jewish writers of the Second Temple period. Apart from 1 Enoch, he appears in Jubilees, in the Dead Sea Scrolls, and in later works such as 2 Enoch (preserved in Slavonic) and 3 Enoch (Hebrew). He was celebrated as a scribe, a sage, a recipient of heavenly secrets, and a witness to the coming judgment. Jude’s readers, who knew the Watcher story (v. 6) and probably the nature images of 1 Enoch (vv. 12–13), evidently regarded Enoch as a significant voice.

Fluid boundaries. In Jude’s day, the boundaries of the Jewish scriptures were not settled in every detail, especially at the margins of the “Writings.” The Qumran community preserved many copies of 1 Enoch and Jubilees alongside biblical books. Other Jewish groups, and later the rabbinic tradition, did not treat these books as Scripture. Early Christians inherited this situation, and their own canonical lists took several centuries to stabilize. Jude’s quotation should be read against that background, not anachronistically against later definitions.

Prophecy as present-tense interpretation. Jewish interpreters of the period frequently read ancient prophecies as referring to their own time. The Dead Sea Scrolls’ commentaries (the pesharim) quote a prophetic text and then explain its “interpretation” in terms of the community’s experience. Jude’s “Enoch … prophesied to these” is the same kind of reading.

F. Connections Across Scripture

Genesis 5:21–24; Hebrews 11:5 (Enoch). The biblical basis for Enoch’s reputation. Hebrews celebrates his faith and translation.

Deuteronomy 33:2 (source of the imagery). The coming of the LORD “[with] myriads of holy ones” at Sinai is the ultimate source of the picture in 1 Enoch 1:9 and Jude 14.

Zechariah 14:5; Daniel 7:10 (shared theme). The coming of God with his holy ones to judge.

Matthew 25:31; Mark 8:38; 1 Thessalonians 3:13; 2 Thessalonians 1:7 (shared theme). The return of Jesus with his angels. If Jude understands “the Lord” in the quotation to be Jesus, these texts are his closest New Testament parallels.

Acts 17:28; Titus 1:12–13; 1 Corinthians 15:33; 2 Timothy 3:8 (method). New Testament quotations of, and allusions to, non-biblical sources. These help frame the question of what Jude’s quotation implies.

John 16:8 (vocabulary). The Spirit “will convict” (ἐλέγξει, elenxei) the world, the same verb as Jude’s “to convict.”

G. Scholarly and Theological Questions

1. Did Jude regard 1 Enoch as Scripture? Discussed in the deep dive. Interpreters remain divided. The most cautious conclusion is that Jude regarded this prophecy as a true word of God about judgment, and that his readers recognized it as authoritative. The question of what Jude thought about the book as a whole cannot be answered from his single quotation.

2. Who is “the Lord” in v. 14? God in 1 Enoch. Most likely Jesus in Jude’s application, given Jude’s usage elsewhere and the New Testament’s expectation of Christ’s coming with his angels.

3. Which text did Jude use? Probably a Greek or Aramaic form of 1 Enoch close to the ones we know, perhaps with his own adaptations. The exact relationship is debated.

4. Which reading is original in v. 15? “All the impious” (SBLGNT), “all their impious ones” (Byzantine), or “every soul” (NA28). The meaning is the same in substance.

5. Does Jude’s use of Enoch undermine his authority? Some early Christians thought so. The church as a whole concluded otherwise. Modern readers who share the church’s acceptance of Jude can read his quotation as an example of how the Spirit used the literature and traditions of the time to speak a true word, without thereby endorsing everything in that literature.

H. The Passage Within Jude

The Enoch quotation is the centerpiece of Jude’s case. It supplies the clearest example of the judgment “written beforehand” (v. 4), and its fourfold “impious” confirms that Jude’s description of the intruders was the language of prophecy. It is followed by another application (“These are…,” v. 16), which applies the prophecy’s charge of “harsh words” to the intruders’ grumbling and arrogant speech.

The quotation also provides the letter’s main picture of the future: the Lord coming with his holy ones. The readers, by contrast, are told to wait for “the mercy of our Lord Jesus Christ” (v. 21) and are promised that God will set them “in the presence of His glory unblemished” (v. 24). The same coming that brings judgment on the impious brings mercy and joy to those who have been kept.

I. Contemporary Understanding

Suggested applications, distinct from the original meaning.

Reading Scripture with its world. Jude’s quotation of Enoch shows that the New Testament was written in conversation with the literature and traditions of its time. Modern readers may find their understanding enriched, not threatened, by learning about that world: the Dead Sea Scrolls, the Jewish writings of the Second Temple period, and the history of the canon.

Humility about the canon. Christians today hold different views about which books belong in the Old Testament (Protestant, Catholic, Orthodox, and Ethiopian canons differ). Jude’s quotation of Enoch, and the varied responses of Tertullian, Jerome, Augustine, and Calvin, may encourage both conviction about one’s own tradition and charity toward others.

Words matter to God. Enoch’s prophecy convicts sinners not only of their deeds but of “the harsh [words]” they have spoken against God. Jude’s letter is full of concern about speech: slander, harsh words, grumbling, boasting, scoffing. In an age of easy and often harsh public speech, the passage may prompt reflection on accountability for words.

The coming of the Lord. The picture of the Lord coming with countless holy ones is both a warning and a comfort. It warns that injustice and ungodliness will not have the last word. It comforts those who wait for “the mercy of our Lord Jesus Christ” (v. 21).

J. Reflection and Discussion

  1. How did you react to learning that Jude quotes a book not found in most Bibles? Did it raise questions for you, and how would you begin to answer them?
  2. Compare Jude’s use of Enoch with Paul’s quotations of Greek poets (Acts 17:28; Titus 1:12; 1 Cor 15:33). What similarities and differences do you see?
  3. Tertullian, Jerome, Augustine, and Calvin each responded differently to Jude’s quotation. Which response do you find most persuasive, and why?
  4. Enoch’s prophecy emphasizes “harsh [words] … spoken against Him.” Why might speech be singled out alongside deeds in a description of ungodliness?
  5. How does the picture of the Lord coming “with myriads of His holy ones” shape your hope and your sense of responsibility?

K. Sources

  • Tertullian, De cultu feminarum 1.3 (ANF 4); Jerome, De viris illustribus 4 (NPNF² 3); Augustine, City of God 15.23 (NPNF¹ 2); Calvin, Commentaries on the Catholic Epistles, on Jude 14.
  • On the text and canonical status of 1 Enoch: “Book of Enoch,” Wikipedia (accessed 7 November 2026); George W. E. Nickelsburg, 1 Enoch 1 (Hermeneia, 2001); J. T. Milik, The Books of Enoch (1976) (both cited from prior knowledge).
  • Bauckham, Jude, 2 Peter, on vv. 14–15 (cited from prior knowledge).

Chapter 9. Grumblers, Fault-Finders, and Flatterers (Jude 16)

A. The Text

16 These are discontented grumblers, walking according to their own lusts; and their mouth speaks swollen words, giving admiration to persons for the sake of profit;

— Jude 16, LSV

B. Plain-English Orientation

After Enoch’s prophecy, Jude applies it once more: “These are…” Enoch’s prophecy ended with “the harsh [words] that impious sinners spoke against Him.” Jude now shows how the intruders’ speech fits that description. They grumble, complaining against God and their circumstances. They blame their lot, always dissatisfied. They follow their own desires. They speak arrogant words, boastful and grandiose. And they flatter people when there is something to be gained.

The portrait is psychologically sharp. The same people who complain about God and boast about themselves are servile toward anyone who can benefit them. Their speech moves between resentment, arrogance, and flattery, and self-interest drives all three.

C. Phrase-by-Phrase Exploration

“These are discontented grumblers”

The verse opens with the formula Οὗτοί εἰσιν (Houtoi eisin), “These are,” which marks the third application in the letter (vv. 8, 10, 12, 16, 19). The LSV’s “discontented grumblers” combines two Greek words, each found nowhere else in the New Testament.

γογγυσταί (gongystai), “grumblers, murmurers.” The noun comes from the verb γογγύζω (gongyzō), “to grumble, mutter,” whose sound imitates low, discontented muttering. The noun itself is rare, and in the Greek Old Testament it appears only in later revisions.129 The verb and its related noun γογγυσμός (gongysmos), however, are the standard Septuagint terms for Israel’s murmuring in the wilderness. “In YHWH’s hearing your murmurings, which you are murmuring against Him … Your murmurings [are] not against us, but against YHWH” (Exod 16:8, LSV). After the spies’ report, “all the sons of Israel murmur against Moses and against Aaron” (Num 14:2, LSV), and God says, “The murmurings of the sons of Israel, which they are murmuring against Me, I have heard” (Num 14:27, LSV). The murmuring led to the death of that generation in the wilderness, the very event Jude recalled in v. 5. Korah’s rebellion, too, was a “murmuring” against Aaron (Num 16:11; 17:5).

Paul draws the same lesson for the church: “neither murmur, as also some of them murmured, and perished by the destroyer” (1 Cor 10:10, LSV). He urges the Philippians, “Do all things without murmurings and deliberations” (Phil 2:14, LSV). For Jude’s readers, “grumblers” would have called up the whole wilderness story: people delivered by God who complained against him and perished.

μεμψίμοιροι (mempsimoiroi), “fault-finders, complaining of their lot.” This word combines μέμφομαι (memphomai, “to blame, find fault”) and μοῖρα (moira, “lot, portion, fate”). Its sense is “blaming one’s lot,” “querulous,” and it describes a person perpetually dissatisfied with what life, or God, has given.130 It was a recognized character type in Greek moral writing. The philosopher Theophrastus (fourth century BC) included “the Grumbler” (Μεμψιμοιρία, Mempsimoiria) among his thirty sketches of human types. His grumbler receives a gift of food from a friend’s table and complains, “He grudged me a dish of soup and a cup of wine, I suppose, and so couldn’t invite me to dinner.”131 Jude’s readers would have recognized the type, a person whose ingratitude turns every blessing into a grievance.

Put together, the two words describe people whose fundamental posture toward God is complaint. They are the opposite of the thankful, contented trust that the faith produces (Phil 4:11–12; 1 Tim 6:6–8).

  1. Abbott-Smith, s.v. γογγυστής: “[in Sm.: Pr 26:22, Is 29:24; Th.: Pr 26:20] a murmurer: Ju 16” (i.e., found in the later Greek versions of Symmachus and Theodotion, not in the main Septuagint).↩︎

  2. Abbott-Smith, s.v. μεμψίμοιρος: “(< μέμφομαι, + μοῖρα, fate, lot), complaining of one’s fate, querulous: Ju 16.”↩︎

  3. Theophrastus, Characters 17, as rendered in Charles E. Bennett and William A. Hammond, The Characters of Theophrastus (New York: Longmans, Green, 1902), Project Gutenberg #58242. Bennett and Hammond title the sketch “The Thankless Man.” The Greek title is Μεμψιμοιρία.↩︎

“walking according to their own lusts”

The phrase κατὰ τὰς ἐπιθυμίας αὐτῶν πορευόμενοι (kata tas epithymias autōn poreuomenoi), “going according to their desires,” describes a whole way of life governed by craving. The verb πορεύομαι (poreuomai), “to go, walk,” repeats the “went” of v. 11 (“they went on in the way of Cain”), and it will return in v. 18 (“following after their own lusts of impieties”). The noun ἐπιθυμία (epithymia), “desire, craving,” is neutral in itself but in this context means disordered desire. The intruders’ grumbling and their self-indulgence are two sides of the same thing. Those whose desires rule them will always complain that their desires are not met.

A textual note. The SBLGNT and the Byzantine text read αὐτῶν (autōn), “their,” while NA28 reads ἑαυτῶν (heautōn), “their own.” The difference is minimal. The LSV’s “their own” matches the sense of either.132

  1. eBible.org SBLGNT notes: “αὐτῶν WH Treg RP ] ἑαυτῶν NA28.”↩︎

“and their mouth speaks swollen words”

The Greek τὸ στόμα αὐτῶν λαλεῖ ὑπέρογκα (to stoma autōn lalei hyperonka) means literally “their mouth speaks swollen things.” The adjective ὑπέρογκος (hyperonkos), from ὑπέρ (hyper, “over”) and ὄγκος (onkos, “bulk, mass”), means “excessively large, immoderate,” and in later Greek it was used of arrogant, bombastic speech.133 The LSV’s “swollen words” is vivid and accurate. The ASV’s “great swelling words” and the BSB’s “arrogantly” capture the sense.

The phrase has a striking biblical precedent. In the Greek version of Daniel, the arrogant king of the end time “shall speak swollen things” (λαλήσει ὑπέρογκα, lalēsei hyperonka) against the God of gods (Dan 11:36, Theodotion).134 The Hebrew behind it says he “speaks wonderful things against the God of gods” (Dan 11:36, LSV). Daniel also describes the little horn with “a mouth speaking great things” (Dan 7:8, LSV). 1 Enoch, which Jude has just quoted, accuses sinners of speaking “proud and hard words / With your impure mouths against His greatness” (1 Enoch 5:4) and “proud and insolent words against His righteousness” (101:3).135 Jude’s phrase places the intruders’ boasting in this line of arrogant speech against God.

What did their “swollen words” sound like? Perhaps grand claims about their visions (v. 8), their freedom (v. 4), or their spiritual superiority (v. 19). Second Peter’s parallel adds content: “speaking swollen words of vanity, they entice in desires of the flesh” (2 Pet 2:18, LSV). Their impressive talk was a means of luring others into their way of life.

  1. Abbott-Smith, s.v. ὑπέρογκος: “of excessive weight or size; metaph., excessive, immoderate, in late writers, of arrogant speech (v. Mayor on Ju, l.c.): n. pl., II Pe 2:18, Ju 16.”↩︎

  2. Greek Daniel (Theodotion) 11:36: καὶ λαλήσει ὑπέρογκα (Brenton’s Greek Septuagint, eBible.org).↩︎

  3. 1 Enoch 5:4; 101:3, trans. R. H. Charles (1917), Wikisource.↩︎

“giving admiration to persons”

The phrase θαυμάζοντες πρόσωπα (thaumazontes prosōpa) means literally “admiring faces.” It is not natural Greek. It is a Hebrew idiom rendered word for word in the Greek Old Testament. The Hebrew expression “to lift up the face” of someone (nāśāʾ pānîm) means to show favor or partiality, especially to the powerful. The Septuagint often translated it as θαυμάζειν πρόσωπον, “to admire a face.”136 For example:

  • “you do not lift up the face of the poor, nor honor the face of the great” (Lev 19:15, LSV), where the Septuagint has “you shall not admire the face of the powerful”;
  • “your God YHWH … who does not accept by appearances, nor takes a bribe” (Deut 10:17, LSV), where the Septuagint has “who does not admire a face”;
  • “[It] is not good to discern faces” (Prov 28:21, LSV);
  • and, strikingly, in the Sodom narrative itself, the angel’s words to Lot, “Behold, I have also accepted your face for this thing” (Gen 19:21, LSV), where the Septuagint has ἐθαύμασά σου τὸ πρόσωπον, “I have admired your face,” meaning “I have granted your request.”

The New Testament uses a related compound for the same vice: “do not hold the faith of the glory of our Lord Jesus Christ in favor by appearance” (Jas 2:1, LSV); “if you favor by appearance, you work sin” (Jas 2:9, LSV). Peter at Cornelius’s house declares that “God is not favoring by appearance” (Acts 10:34, LSV).

The intruders, then, show favoritism. They flatter and court people of influence.

  1. Brenton’s Greek Septuagint: Gen 19:21 (ἐθαύμασά σου τὸ πρόσωπον); Lev 19:15 (οὐδὲ μὴ θαυμάσῃς πρόσωπον δυνάστου); Deut 10:17 (οὐ θαυμάζει πρόσωπον); 2 Chr 19:7; Job 13:10; 22:8; 32:22; Prov 18:5; Isa 9:15 (versification varies).↩︎

“for the sake of profit”

The reason for their flattery is ὠφελείας χάριν (ōpheleias charin), “for the sake of advantage” or “for profit.” The noun ὠφέλεια (ōpheleia) means “help, benefit, advantage.”137 The word χάριν (charin), used here as a preposition meaning “for the sake of,” is in origin the accusative of χάρις (charis), “grace.” It is tempting to see an irony: the intruders, who turned the grace (χάρις) of God into license (v. 4), now flatter people for the sake of (χάριν) profit. Whether Jude intended the echo is uncertain, since χάριν was an everyday preposition, but attentive readers may have heard it.

The combination is telling. Those who grumble against God and speak arrogantly against him are servile toward people who can reward them. Paul describes similar figures: “such do not serve our Lord Jesus Christ, but their own belly; and through the good word and fair speech they deceive the hearts of the harmless” (Rom 16:18, LSV). Paul contrasts his own ministry: “at no time did we come with speech of flattery … nor in a pretext for covetousness” (1 Thess 2:5, LSV). Balaam’s “reward” (v. 11) and the self-feeding shepherds (v. 12) find their counterpart here. The intruders’ speech, like their leadership, serves their own advantage.

  1. Abbott-Smith, s.v. ὠφέλεια: “profit, advantage, benefit: Ro 3:1, Ju 16.”↩︎

D. Original-Language Investigation

Greek Transliteration Basic meaning In context Notes
γογγυστής gongystēs grumbler, murmurer “grumblers” NT hapax; cf. γογγύζω, LXX wilderness murmuring.
μεμψίμοιρος mempsimoiros complaining of one’s lot, querulous “discontented” NT hapax; Theophrastus’s “Grumbler” type.
ἐπιθυμία epithymia desire, craving “lusts” Also v. 18.
πορεύομαι poreuomai go, walk “walking” Echoes v. 11; anticipates v. 18.
στόμα stoma mouth “their mouth” Semitic expression.
ὑπέρογκος hyperonkos excessive, swollen; arrogant “swollen words” Dan 11:36 Theod.; 2 Pet 2:18.
θαυμάζω πρόσωπον thaumazō prosōpon “admire a face” = show partiality “giving admiration to persons” LXX idiom for Heb. nāśāʾ pānîm.
ὠφέλεια ōpheleia advantage, profit “profit”
χάριν charin for the sake of “for the sake of” Preposition (from χάρις).

A caution about the “admiring faces” idiom. Because θαυμάζω normally means “to marvel, wonder at,” a reader without the Septuagint background might think the intruders were simply impressed by people. The phrase is a fixed idiom for partiality and favoritism. Here, as often, the Greek of the New Testament makes sense only against the Greek of the Old Testament.

E. The Historical and Cultural World

Patronage and flattery. The Greco-Roman world was structured by patronage. Wealthy patrons supported clients, who repaid them with loyalty, public praise, and services. Flattery was the lubricant of the system, and moralists from Theophrastus to Plutarch wrote about how to tell a flatterer from a friend. Early Christian communities, which met in the homes of wealthier members and depended on their hospitality, were not immune to these dynamics. James warns against honoring the rich man in fine clothes and dishonoring the poor man (Jas 2:1–9). Jude’s intruders seem to have courted influential members of the community, perhaps the hosts of the love feasts, for their own advantage.

Moral character types. Greek moral writers catalogued human vices in sketches of recognizable types, including the flatterer, the grumbler, the boaster, and the toady. Jude’s string of vivid descriptors resembles such sketches, though his categories are shaped by Scripture: the murmurers of the wilderness, the arrogant speakers of Daniel and Enoch, the partial judges condemned by the Law.

F. Connections Across Scripture

Exodus 16; Numbers 14; 1 Corinthians 10:10 (background). The wilderness murmuring is the main background for “grumblers.” Jude’s readers would connect v. 16 with v. 5: those who murmured perished.

Numbers 16:11; 17:5 (Korah). Korah’s rebellion was a “murmuring.” The grumblers of v. 16 walk in Korah’s “controversy” (v. 11).

Daniel 7:8; 11:36 (verbal parallel). The arrogant king who “speaks wonderful things,” Greek “swollen things,” against God provides the closest biblical parallel to “swollen words.”

Leviticus 19:15; Deuteronomy 10:17; James 2:1–9; Acts 10:34 (idiom and theme). Partiality is condemned in the Law, and God himself is the model of impartiality.

Romans 16:17–18; 1 Thessalonians 2:5 (shared theme). Paul’s warnings against those who serve their own appetites with “fair speech,” and his own refusal of flattery, parallel Jude’s charge.

2 Peter 2:18 (literary parallel). “Swollen words of vanity” used to entice others.

1 Enoch 5:4; 101:3 (source tradition). Proud and hard words against God.

G. Scholarly and Theological Questions

1. How does v. 16 relate to the Enoch quotation? Most interpreters see v. 16 as the application of the quotation, especially of its final phrase about “harsh [words] … against Him.” The intruders’ grumbling, boasting, and flattery are the “harsh words” of the prophecy in present form.

2. Whom did the intruders flatter? Jude does not say. Suggestions include wealthy members who hosted the community, people who might become followers and supporters, and church leaders whose approval they sought. The general point, that they cultivated people for gain, does not depend on a specific identification.

3. Is all complaint sinful? Scripture contains many complaints addressed to God, the laments of the Psalms, of Job, and of Jeremiah, which are not condemned. The difference lies in the posture. Lament brings pain to God in trust. Grumbling speaks against God in resentment and unbelief. The wilderness murmuring that Jude recalls was a refusal to trust the God who had delivered them (Num 14:11). Jude’s word μεμψίμοιροι, “blaming one’s lot,” captures this spirit of grievance against God’s providence.

H. The Passage Within Jude

Verse 16 completes the third “text and application” unit (vv. 14–16), applying Enoch’s prophecy to the intruders. It also gathers threads from earlier in the letter: the murmuring of the wilderness generation (v. 5), the slander of glorious ones (vv. 8, 10), Korah’s rebellion (v. 11), and Balaam’s greed (v. 11). And it prepares for v. 18, where the apostles’ prediction of “scoffers, following after their own lusts” repeats the phrase about walking according to lusts.

Throughout this middle section, speech is a recurring concern: slander (vv. 8–10), harsh words (v. 15), grumbling and swollen words (v. 16), and scoffing (v. 18). By contrast, the readers’ speech is to be prayer “in the Holy Spirit” (v. 20) and the letter ends in praise (vv. 24–25).

I. Contemporary Understanding

Suggested applications, distinct from the original meaning.

Gratitude as resistance. Grumbling can become a habit of mind that colors every blessing with grievance, as in Theophrastus’s grumbler who received a gift and complained he had not been invited to dinner. Jude’s portrait may invite believers to examine their own speech about God, their circumstances, and their communities, and to cultivate thankfulness as a spiritual discipline.

Lament vs. grumbling. Christians sometimes suppress honest lament out of fear that it is “grumbling.” Scripture’s laments show that bringing pain to God in faith is not the sin Jude describes. The question is whether complaint is addressed to God in trust or spoken against God in resentment.

Flattery and influence. In churches as in every institution, there are temptations to court those with money, status, or power. James and Jude both condemn partiality. Communities may consider whether they treat wealthy or influential members differently, and whether their leaders’ speech is shaped by the desire for advantage.

Inflated speech. “Swollen words” are common in religious marketing, political rhetoric, and online self-presentation. Jude’s phrase may prompt reflection on the difference between speech that serves truth and speech that serves the speaker’s image.

J. Reflection and Discussion

  1. Why do you think grumbling was treated so seriously in the wilderness narratives and in Jude? What does grumbling reveal about a person’s relationship with God?
  2. How would you distinguish biblical lament from the grumbling Jude condemns? Can you think of examples from Scripture or experience?
  3. Jude describes people who are arrogant toward God and flattering toward people. Why do you think these two traits often go together?
  4. Where do you see the temptation to “admire faces,” to show favoritism for advantage, in your church, workplace, or community?
  5. What practices might help cultivate contentment and gratitude in a culture that often encourages complaint?

K. Sources

  • Abbott-Smith, s.vv. γογγυστής, μεμψίμοιρος, ὑπέρογκος, ὠφέλεια.
  • Theophrastus, Characters 17 (“The Thankless Man”/“Grumbler”), trans. C. E. Bennett and W. A. Hammond (1902), Project Gutenberg #58242.
  • 1 Enoch 5:4; 101:3, trans. R. H. Charles (1917).

Chapter 10. Remember What the Apostles Said (Jude 17–19)

A. The Text

17 but you, beloved, remember the sayings spoken before by the apostles of our Lord Jesus Christ, 18 that they said to you, that in the last time there will be scoffers, following after their own lusts of impieties, 19 these are those causing divisions, natural men, not having the Spirit.

— Jude 17–19, LSV

B. Plain-English Orientation

With “but you, beloved,” Jude turns from the intruders to his readers. He has been building his case from ancient sources: Israel’s history, angelic traditions, the stories of Cain, Balaam, and Korah, and Enoch’s prophecy. Now he brings it close to home. The readers themselves had heard the apostles warn that in the last days there would be scoffers, driven by ungodly desires. The intruders fulfill that prediction. In Jude’s final description of them, they are people who create divisions, who are merely “natural” in their outlook, and who do not have the Spirit.

This is the last “text and application” unit in the letter. Its text is not an ancient prophecy but the recent teaching of the apostles, and its application is the most theologically pointed charge of all: the people who claim spiritual revelation do not have the Spirit.

C. Phrase-by-Phrase Exploration

“but you, beloved”

The Greek Ὑμεῖς δέ, ἀγαπητοί (Hymeis de, agapētoi) places the pronoun “you” first for emphasis: “But you, beloved.” It marks a sharp contrast with the repeated “these” (οὗτοι, houtoi) of the previous verses. This is the second of Jude’s three addresses to the “beloved” (vv. 3, 17, 20), and it begins the turn toward the readers that will carry through to the end of the letter.

“remember”

The aorist imperative μνήσθητε (mnēsthēte), “remember,” is Jude’s first direct command to his readers. In the Bible, remembering is never merely recalling information. It means calling the past into the present so that it shapes action. Moses tells Israel, “you have remembered all the way which your God YHWH has caused you to go these forty years in the wilderness, in order to humble you” (Deut 8:2, LSV). Paul writes, “Remember Jesus Christ, raised out of the dead” (2 Tim 2:8, LSV). Jude began the main body of his letter with “I intend to remind you” (v. 5). Now he asks his readers to do their own remembering.

“the sayings spoken before by the apostles of our Lord Jesus Christ”

The readers are to remember τῶν ῥημάτων τῶν προειρημένων ὑπὸ τῶν ἀποστόλων (tōn rhēmatōn tōn proeirēmenōn hypo tōn apostolōn), “the words spoken beforehand by the apostles.” The perfect participle προειρημένων (proeirēmenōn), “spoken before” or “foretold,” stresses that the warning was given in advance and still stands.

Two observations follow:

  1. Jude distinguishes himself from “the apostles.” He does not say “we apostles.” This fits the author’s modest self-description in v. 1 and is one of the reasons for thinking that the author did not claim apostolic status. Some scholars have read the phrase as evidence that the apostles belonged to a past generation and that the letter is therefore late. The next verse suggests otherwise: the apostles had spoken “to you,” to the readers themselves.
  2. The apostles’ words are placed alongside the prophets. Jude has cited Israel’s history and Enoch’s prophecy. Now he adds the apostles’ teaching as a further authority. Second Peter’s parallel makes the pairing explicit: “to be mindful of the sayings said before by the holy prophets, and of the command of us the apostles of the LORD and Savior” (2 Pet 3:2, LSV). For the early church, the prophets and the apostles together were the foundation of the faith (Eph 2:20).

“that they said to you”

The imperfect ἔλεγον (elegon), “they were saying” or “they used to say,” suggests repeated teaching rather than a single statement. The warning about scoffers was a regular part of apostolic instruction. And it was said ὑμῖν (hymin), “to you.” Jude’s readers had heard it firsthand, probably from the missionaries who founded their community. That detail points to a date within the first generation or so of the church. It also makes Jude’s appeal personal. He is not citing a distant authority. He is reminding them of what they themselves heard.

What did the apostles say? The New Testament preserves several such warnings. Paul told the Ephesian elders: “there will enter in, after my departing, grievous wolves to you, not sparing the flock, and there will arise men of your own selves, speaking perverse things, to draw away the disciples after them” (Acts 20:29–30, LSV). He told Timothy that “in latter times some will depart from the faith” (1 Tim 4:1, LSV) and that “in the last days there will come perilous times, for men will be lovers of themselves, lovers of money, boasters, proud, slanderous … unthankful … lovers of pleasure more than lovers of God, having a form of piety, but having denied its power” (2 Tim 3:1–5, LSV). Jesus himself had warned: “many false prophets will arise, and will lead many astray” (Matt 24:11, LSV); “behold, I have foretold you all things” (Mark 13:23, LSV). Jude’s summary does not quote any of these passages directly, but it reflects the same tradition of prophetic warning.

“that in the last time there will be scoffers”

The phrase ἐπ’ ἐσχάτου χρόνου (ep’ eschatou chronou), “at the last time,” reflects the conviction, shared by the whole New Testament, that the coming of Christ inaugurated the “last days.” Hebrews says God has spoken “in these last days … in [His] Son” (Heb 1:2, LSV). Peter at Pentecost quoted Joel’s promise of what would happen “in the last days” (Acts 2:17). John says, “Little youths, it is the last hour” (1 John 2:18, LSV). The “last time” is not a distant future. It is the period in which the church lives, between Christ’s first coming and his return.

A textual note. The manuscripts differ in the wording of this phrase. The Byzantine text and TR read ἐν ἐσχάτῳ χρόνῳ (en eschatō chronō), “in the last time.” NA27 read ἐπ’ ἐσχάτου τοῦ χρόνου, with the article. SBLGNT and NA28 read ἐπ’ ἐσχάτου χρόνου. Some manuscripts also add a second ὅτι (“that”) before the quotation. The sense is unaffected.138

The scoffers (ἐμπαῖκται, empaiktai) are “mockers,” people who ridicule. The word appears in the New Testament only here and in 2 Pet 3:3.139 In 2 Peter the scoffers mock the promise of Christ’s return: “Where is the promise of His coming? For since the fathers fell asleep, all things so remain from the beginning of the creation” (2 Pet 3:4, LSV). Jude does not specify the object of their mockery. In the context of his letter it is most naturally the moral demands of the faith and the warning of judgment. The intruders treat God’s judgment as a joke, and their way of life follows from it. The “scorner” of Israel’s wisdom literature is a similar figure: “O the blessedness of that one, … And has not sat in the seat of scorners” (Ps 1:1, LSV). Proverbs adds, “Do not reprove a scorner, lest he hate you” (Prov 9:8, LSV).

  1. SBLGNT apparatus and eBible.org SBLGNT notes: “ὑμῖν WH NA28 ] + ὅτι Treg NA27 RP • Ἐπʼ ἐσχάτου χρόνου WH Treg NA28 ] Ἐπʼ ἐσχάτου τοῦ χρόνου NA27; ἐν ἐσχάτῳ χρόνῳ RP.”↩︎

  2. Computed from MorphGNT/SBLGNT. Abbott-Smith, s.v. ἐμπαίκτης: “a mocker: II Pe 3:3, Ju 18,” noting one LXX occurrence (Isa 3:4).↩︎

“following after their own lusts of impieties”

The scoffers are κατὰ τὰς ἑαυτῶν ἐπιθυμίας πορευόμενοι τῶν ἀσεβειῶν (kata tas heautōn epithymias poreuomenoi tōn asebeiōn), literally “going according to their own desires of ungodlinesses.” The genitive “of impieties” describes the kind of desires: ungodly desires. The phrase echoes v. 16 almost word for word (“walking according to their own lusts”) and adds Jude’s key word one last time: ἀσέβεια, “impiety.” From v. 4, through the fourfold repetition in the Enoch quotation (v. 15), to here, the intruders are consistently the “ungodly.”

“these are those causing divisions”

The final application begins with the familiar formula, Οὗτοί εἰσιν (Houtoi eisin), “These are.” The first description is οἱ ἀποδιορίζοντες (hoi apodiorizontes). The verb, found only here in the New Testament, means “to mark off, separate, make distinctions.”140 It is built from ὅρος (horos, “boundary”) with prefixes meaning “away” and “through.” The intruders draw lines through the community.

Two senses are possible:

  1. “Those who cause divisions.” The intruders create factions, separating their followers from the rest of the community. Paul warns against “those who are causing the divisions and the stumbling-blocks, contrary to the teaching that you learned” (Rom 16:17, LSV), and lists “dissensions, sects” among the works of the flesh (Gal 5:20, LSV).
  2. “Those who make distinctions.” The intruders classify people into spiritual ranks, regarding themselves as the truly “spiritual” and others as inferior. This reading fits the next word, ψυχικοί, especially if that was a term the intruders used for others.

The two senses overlap. Drawing distinctions of spiritual rank is a way of dividing a community. The Textus Receptus adds ἑαυτούς (heautous), “themselves,” which gives the KJV’s “These be they who separate themselves,” as though the intruders withdrew from the community. Most manuscripts and the critical editions lack the word.141 Jude’s picture is not of people who have left but of people who divide from within. They still sit at the love feasts (v. 12).

  1. Abbott-Smith, s.v. ἀποδιορίζω: “to mark off, hence metaph. to make separations: Ju 19.”↩︎

  2. The TR (Scrivener) reads οἱ ἀποδιορίζοντες ἑαυτούς. The Text-Critical Greek NT notes give the reading with ἑαυτούς in about 21% of manuscripts. Clement of Alexandria paraphrased: “Those, he says, separating the faithful from the unfaithful” (Adumbrationes on Jude 19, ANF 2).↩︎

“natural men”

The adjective ψυχικοί (psychikoi) is difficult to translate. It derives from ψυχή (psychē), “soul, life,” and describes what belongs to ordinary, natural human life as opposed to what belongs to the Spirit of God.142 The LSV has “natural men,” the KJV “sensual,” the WEB “worldly-minded,” and the BSB “worldly.”

Paul uses the word in a similar contrast: “the natural man does not receive the things of the Spirit of God, for they are foolishness to him … and he who is spiritual, indeed discerns all things” (1 Cor 2:14–15, LSV). He also contrasts the “natural body” with the “spiritual body” of the resurrection (1 Cor 15:44). James describes a false wisdom as “earthly, physical, demon-like” (Jas 3:15, LSV), where “physical” translates the same Greek word.

The term carries a sharp irony. If the intruders claimed spiritual superiority through their dreams and visions, and perhaps used “natural” (psychikoi) as a label for less enlightened believers, Jude turns their own category back on them. They are the merely natural ones. A century later, some Gnostic groups divided humanity into “spiritual,” “natural” (psychikoi), and “material” classes. Jude’s opponents were not second-century Gnostics, and it would be anachronistic to read that system into the letter. The Corinthian correspondence shows, however, that the contrast between “natural” and “spiritual” people was already being used in early Christian disputes about status.

  1. Abbott-Smith, s.v. ψυχικός: “of the ψυχή (as the lower part of the immaterial in man), EV, natural: opp. to πνευματικός, I Co 2:14 15:44, 46; πνεῦμα μὴ ἔχοντες … Ju 19.”↩︎

“not having the Spirit”

The final phrase, πνεῦμα μὴ ἔχοντες (pneuma mē echontes), “not having the Spirit,” is the most serious charge in the letter. In the New Testament, possessing the Holy Spirit is the mark of belonging to Christ. Paul writes: “if anyone does not have the Spirit of Christ—this one is not His” (Rom 8:9, LSV), and “in one Spirit we were all immersed into one body” (1 Cor 12:13, LSV).

The intruders, who appear to have claimed special spiritual experiences, are in Jude’s judgment devoid of the Spirit. Their dreams (v. 8) were not the Spirit’s gift. Their conduct, with its immorality, greed, grumbling, arrogance, and divisiveness, is the opposite of the fruit of the Spirit. The very next verse urges the readers to pray “in the Holy Spirit” (v. 20). Jude’s contrast is complete: the intruders lack the Spirit, while the readers pray in the Spirit.

(The Greek word πνεῦμα has no article here and could in principle mean “spirit” in a general sense: “not having a spirit,” that is, being merely soulish and lacking a spiritual dimension. Given the New Testament usage and the contrast with v. 20, most interpreters take it as the Holy Spirit.)

D. Original-Language Investigation

Greek Transliteration Basic meaning In context Notes
ὑμεῖς δέ hymeis de but you “but you” Emphatic contrast with “these.”
μιμνῄσκομαι mimnēskomai remember “remember” Aorist imperative.
ῥῆμα rhēma word, saying “the sayings”
προλέγω prolegō say beforehand, foretell “spoken before” Perfect passive participle.
ἀπόστολος apostolos apostle, sent one “the apostles” Jude does not include himself.
ἔλεγον elegon they were saying “they said” Imperfect: repeated teaching.
ἔσχατος χρόνος eschatos chronos last time “the last time” Variants in wording.
ἐμπαίκτης empaiktēs mocker, scoffer “scoffers” Only here and 2 Pet 3:3.
ἐπιθυμία epithymia desire “lusts” Repeats v. 16.
ἀσέβεια asebeia impiety “impieties” Final use of the key word.
ἀποδιορίζω apodiorizō mark off, make divisions “causing divisions” NT hapax. TR adds ἑαυτούς.
ψυχικός psychikos natural, soulish, unspiritual “natural men” 1 Cor 2:14; 15:44; Jas 3:15.
πνεῦμα pneuma spirit, Spirit “the Spirit” The Holy Spirit (cf. v. 20).

A caution about ψυχικός. It is tempting to build a doctrine of human nature, body, soul, and spirit, on words like ψυχικός. That is not Jude’s purpose, nor Paul’s in 1 Corinthians. The word describes an orientation: life lived on the level of ordinary human impulses, without the transforming presence of God’s Spirit. It does not mean that the soul is bad or that the Spirit replaces human nature.

E. The Historical and Cultural World

Apostolic teaching and its transmission. In the first decades of the church, the apostles and their associates traveled among the churches teaching, and their teaching was passed on orally before and alongside written letters. Jude’s readers had heard apostolic warnings “said to you.” The phrase gives a glimpse of the early process by which communities received, remembered, and relied on the apostles’ words, the same process that eventually led to the collection of the New Testament writings.

Factions and spiritual elitism. The churches of the first century were small communities in which differences of background, status, and spiritual experience could easily harden into factions. The Corinthian church divided around favored teachers (“I, indeed, am of Paul,” 1 Cor 3:4, LSV) and around spiritual gifts (1 Cor 12–14). Jude’s intruders seem to have created a similar division, with their followers presented as the truly spiritual.

“Last days” expectation. Jewish apocalyptic thought expected a period of intensified evil and apostasy before God’s final intervention. Early Christians believed that this period had begun with Christ, and they interpreted false teaching and moral collapse within the church as signs of it (1 John 2:18–19; 2 Tim 3:1–5). Jude shares that outlook.

F. Connections Across Scripture

2 Peter 3:1–4 (literary parallel). Peter’s version of this passage pairs the prophets and the apostles and specifies that the scoffers mock the promise of Christ’s coming. The relationship between the two letters is especially close here.

Acts 20:29–30; 1 Timothy 4:1; 2 Timothy 3:1–5; 4:3–4 (apostolic predictions). These passages illustrate the kind of warning Jude’s readers had heard. Note especially 2 Tim 4:3: people “will heap up teachers according to their own desires” (LSV).

Matthew 24:11, 24; Mark 13:22–23 (dominical predictions). Jesus’ warnings about false prophets in the last days are the root of the apostolic tradition.

1 John 2:18–19 (shared situation). John’s opponents “went forth out of us, but they were not of us” (1 John 2:19, LSV). Jude’s intruders, by contrast, are still within the community, causing division from inside.

1 Corinthians 2:14–15; 15:44–46; James 3:15 (vocabulary). The contrast between the “natural” and the “spiritual.”

Romans 8:9; 1 Corinthians 12:13 (theology of the Spirit). Having the Spirit is the mark of belonging to Christ.

Romans 16:17; 1 Corinthians 1:10; 3:3–4; Galatians 5:20 (divisions). Paul’s warnings against division illuminate Jude’s charge.

G. Scholarly and Theological Questions

1. Does v. 17 show that Jude is late? Some argue that the reference to “the apostles” as a group whose words are remembered implies a post-apostolic date. Others point out that v. 18 says the apostles spoke “to you,” the readers, which places the letter within the lifetime of people who had heard the apostles. The verse is compatible with an early or a later date, but it does not require a late one.

2. Which apostles? Jude does not name them. The readers may have been evangelized by Paul, by Peter, or by other apostles and missionaries. Since the warning is generic, the question cannot be settled.

3. Did the intruders use the term ψυχικοί? It is an attractive hypothesis, especially given the parallel in 1 Corinthians, but it cannot be proven from Jude alone. What is certain is that Jude uses the term to deny the intruders’ spiritual claims.

4. Can someone in the church “not have the Spirit”? Jude’s judgment raises a pastoral and theological question. Christian traditions differ on how to understand people who are outwardly part of the church but show no evidence of the Spirit’s work. Some understand them as never having been truly regenerate. Others see them as having fallen away, and others again allow for degrees of unfaithfulness. Jude does not offer a theory. His purpose is to warn his readers not to mistake the intruders’ claims for spiritual reality. Notably, he still holds out hope of rescue for those influenced by them (vv. 22–23).

H. The Passage Within Jude

Verses 17–19 complete the long central section that began in v. 5. They form the fifth “text and application” unit, and the pattern of the whole section is now visible:

Text Application
Three examples of judgment (vv. 5–7) “those dreaming…” (v. 8)
Michael and the Devil (v. 9) “these … speak evil” (v. 10)
Cain, Balaam, Korah (v. 11) “These are stains…” (vv. 12–13)
Enoch’s prophecy (vv. 14–15) “These are … grumblers” (v. 16)
The apostles’ prediction (vv. 17–18) “these are those causing divisions” (v. 19)

The texts move from the distant past to the recent past, from the exodus to the readers’ own memory of the apostles. The last application is the climax: the intruders lack the Spirit. That sets up the contrast with which the next section opens: “But you, beloved, … praying in the Holy Spirit” (v. 20).

I. Contemporary Understanding

Suggested applications, distinct from the original meaning.

The discipline of remembering. Jude does not tell his readers to figure out new strategies. He tells them to remember. Christian communities today may find stability in recalling what they have received, in Scripture, creeds, and the teaching of faithful predecessors, rather than in perpetual novelty.

Warnings as gifts. The apostles warned their converts about future false teachers. Warning is an act of love. Leaders and parents who prepare others for difficulty are doing apostolic work.

Spiritual elitism. The intruders seem to have divided the community into the spiritual and the merely natural. Every era has its versions: groups that claim superior experience, knowledge, or holiness and look down on ordinary believers. Jude’s verdict, that such dividers may themselves be “natural” and “not having the Spirit,” is a warning against spiritual pride in every form, including in those who oppose the intruders.

The evidence of the Spirit. Jude’s charge that the intruders lack the Spirit invites a question for every believer: what is the evidence of the Spirit’s presence? The New Testament answers with love, joy, peace, patience, kindness, goodness, faithfulness, gentleness, self-control (Gal 5:22–23), unity (Eph 4:3), and prayer (Jude 20), rather than with dramatic experiences alone.

J. Reflection and Discussion

  1. Jude tells his readers to “remember.” What specific teachings or warnings do you think Christians today most need to remember?
  2. The apostles warned that “scoffers” would come. What forms does scoffing at faith take today, both outside and inside the church?
  3. How can a community distinguish between healthy diversity and divisive factionalism?
  4. What does it mean to “have the Spirit”? How would you recognize the Spirit’s presence in a person or community?
  5. Spiritual elitism divided Jude’s community. Where might spiritual pride show up in your own context, perhaps even among those who consider themselves defenders of the faith?

K. Sources
